The **history of Iran** (also known as [Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Name_of_Iran)) is intertwined with [Greater Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Iran), a region encompassing all areas that have witnessed significant settlement or influence by the [Iranian peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_peoples) and [Iranian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_languages) — chiefly the [Persians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persians) and the [Persian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_language). Central to this region is the [Iranian plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_plateau), now covered by modern [Iran](14653.md). The most pronounced impact of Iranian history can be seen stretching from [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia) in the west to the [Indus Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_River) in the east, including the [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant), [Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia), the [Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus), and majority of [Central Asia](6742.md). It also stands in connection with the histories of other major civilizations, such as [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_India), [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_China), [Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece), [Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Rome), and [Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egypt).

Iran is home to one of the world's oldest continuous civilizations, with historical and urban settlements dating back to the 5th millennium BC.[^cite_note-Brit_AncientIran-1] The Iranian plateau's western regions were home to the [Elamites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elam), [Kassites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kassites), [Gutians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gutians), [Urartians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urartu),[^cite_note-2][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIranica_Online_-_Denḵa_Tepe-3][^cite_note-4] and the [Mannaeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mannaea) in [Kurdistan](61733686.md).[^cite_note-EIO_ziwiye-5][^cite_note-6][^cite_note-7][^cite_note-8][^cite_note-9][^cite_note-10][^cite_note-11] The [Iranian empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_Empire_\(disambiguation\)) began with the rise of the [Medes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medes) during the [Iron Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Age), when Iran was unified as a nation under the [Median kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median_kingdom) in the 7th century BC.[^cite_note-12] By 550 BC, the Medes were sidelined by the conquests of [Cyrus the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrus_the_Great), who brought the Persians to power with the establishment of the [Achaemenid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire). Cyrus' ensuing campaigns enabled the Persian expansion across most of [West Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Asia) and much of Central Asia, and his successors would conquer parts of [Southeast Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeastern_Europe) and [North Africa](21714.md) to preside over the [largest empire yet seen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_empires#Timeline_of_largest_empires_at_the_time). In the 4th century BC, the Achaemenid Empire was conquered by the [Macedonian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonia_\(ancient_kingdom\)) of [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great), whose death led to the establishment of the [Seleucid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucid_Empire) over the bulk of former Achaemenid territory. In the following century, Greek rule of the Iranian plateau came to an end with the rise of the [Parthian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parthian_Empire), which conquered large parts of the Seleucids' Anatolian, Mesopotamian, and [Central Asian](6742.md) holdings. While the Parthians were succeeded by the [Sasanian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sasanian_Empire) in the 2nd century, Iran remained a leading power for the next millennium, although most of this period was marked by the [Roman–Persian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman%E2%80%93Persian_wars).

In the 7th century, the [Muslim conquest of Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_Persia) resulted in the Sasanian Empire's annexation by the [Rashidun Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashidun_Caliphate) and the beginning of the [Islamization of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamization_of_Iran). In spite of invasions by foreign powers, such as the [Greeks](42056.md), [Arabs](2185.md), [Turks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_peoples), and [Mongols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_conquests), Iranian national identity was preserved, allowing it to develop as a distinct political and cultural entity, influencing its invaders. While the [early Muslim conquests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Muslim_conquests) had caused the decline of [Zoroastrianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoroastrianism), which had been Iran's majority religion, the achievements of Iranian civilizations were absorbed into the nascent [Islamic empires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate) and expanded upon during the [Islamic Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Golden_Age). Nomadic Turkic tribes overran parts of the Iranian plateau during the [Late Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Middle_Ages) and [early modern period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_modern_period), negatively impacting the region.[^cite_note-13] By 1501, however, the nation was reunified by the [Safavid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_dynasty), which initiated Iranian history's most momentous religious change since the original Muslim conquest, by [converting Iran to Shia Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_conversion_of_Iran_to_Shia_Islam).[^cite_note-EncIslamica_1993_VII54-14][^cite_note-15] Iran again emerged as a world power, especially in rivalry with the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md). In the 19th century, Iran came into conflict with the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire), which annexed the [South Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Caucasus) by the end of the [Russo-Persian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Persian_Wars).[^cite_note-books.google.nl1-16]

In the 1940s, there were hopes Iran could become a constitutional monarchy, but a [1953 coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1953_Iranian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) aided by the US and UK removed the elected prime minister, and Iran was ruled as an autocracy under the Shah with American support. The monarchy lasted until the [Iranian Revolution](347268.md) in 1979, when the country was declared an [Islamic republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_republic).[^cite_note-Brit_Iran-17][^cite_note-EB_Afary_2026-18] Since then, it has experienced significant political, social, and economic changes. The establishment of an Islamic republic led to a restructuring of the country's political system. Iran's foreign relations have been shaped by regional conflicts, beginning with the [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War) and persisting through many [Arab countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_world); ongoing tensions with [Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Israel_proxy_conflict), the [US](310477.md), and the [Western world](21208200.md); and the [Iranian nuclear program](721807.md), which has been contentious in international diplomacy. Despite [international sanctions](25699056.md) and [internal challenges](16652449.md), Iran remains a key player in regional and global geopolitics.

## Prehistory

Further information: [List of archaeological sites in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_archaeological_sites_in_Iran) and [Prehistory of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistory_of_Iran)

Further information: [Shahr-e Sukhteh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahr-e_Sukhteh)

### Paleolithic

The earliest archaeological artifacts in Iran were found in the [Kashafrud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashafrud) and [Ganj Par](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganj_Par) sites that are thought to date back to 100,000 years ago in the [Middle Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Paleolithic).[^cite_note-Brit_AncientIran-1] [Mousterian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mousterian) stone tools made by [Neanderthals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neanderthal) have also been found.[^cite_note-19] There are more cultural remains of Neanderthals dating back to the Middle Paleolithic period, which mainly have been found in the [Zagros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zagros_Mountains) region (and fewer in central Iran) at sites such as Kobeh, Kunji, [Bisitun Cave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bisitun_Cave), Tamtama, [Warwasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warwasi), and [Yafteh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yafteh) Cave.[^cite_note-20] In 1949, a Neanderthal [radius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radius_\(bone\)) was discovered by [Carleton S. Coon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carleton_S._Coon) in Bisitun Cave.[^cite_note-21] Evidence for [Upper Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Paleolithic) and [Epipaleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epipaleolithic) periods are known mainly from the Zagros Mountains in the caves of [Kermanshah](1489244.md) and [Khorramabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khorramabad) and a few number of sites in [Piranshahr](30865712.md), [Alborz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alborz) and [Central Iran](36063822.md). During this time, people began creating [rock art in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_art_in_Iran).[^cite_note-22][^cite_note-23]

### Neolithic to Chalcolithic

See also: [Iranian hunter-gatherers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_hunter-gatherers) and [Indo-Iranians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Iranians)

Early agricultural communities such as [Chogha Golan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chogha_Golan) in the 11th millennium BC[^cite_note-24][^cite_note-25] along with settlements such as [Chogha Bonut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chogha_Bonut) (the earliest village in Elam) in the 9th millennium BC[^cite_note-26][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIranica_Online_-_Neolithic_Age_In_Iran-27] began to flourish in and around the Zagros Mountains.[^cite_note-MMA_IranTLine_1-28] Around about the same time, the earliest-known clay vessels and modelled human and animal terracotta figurines were produced at [Ganj Dareh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganj_Dareh).[^cite_note-MMA_IranTLine_1-28] There are 10,000-year-old human and animal figurines from Tepe Sarab in [Kermanshah province](448078.md) among many other ancient artefacts.[^cite_note-29]

The south-western part of Iran was part of the [Fertile Crescent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertile_Crescent) where most of humanity's first major crops were grown, in villages such as [Susa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susa) (where a settlement was first founded possibly as early as 4395 BC)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPotts1999\[httpsarchiveorgdetailsarchaeologyofela0000pottpage46mode1upq4395_46–7\]-30] and settlements such as [Chogha Mish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chogha_Mish), dating back to 6800 BC;[^cite_note-xinhuaNet_10082007-31][^cite_note-32] there are 7,000-year-old jars of wine excavated in the Zagros Mountains[^cite_note-33] (now on display at the [University of Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Pennsylvania)) and ruins of 7,000-year-old settlements such as [Tepe Sialk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tepe_Sialk) are further testament to that. The two main Neolithic Iranian settlements were Ganj Dareh and the hypothetical [Zayandeh River Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zayandeh_River_Culture).[^cite_note-34]

### Bronze Age

Further information: [Mannaea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mannaea), [Kura–Araxes culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kura%E2%80%93Araxes_culture), [Akkadian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akkadian_Empire), and [Kassites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kassites)



Cylinder with a ritual scene, early 2nd millennium BC, [Geoy Tepe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geoy_Tepe), Iran



[Chogha Zanbil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chogha_Zanbil) is one of the few extant [ziggurats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ziggurat) outside of [Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia) and is considered to be the best preserved example in the world.

The [Kura–Araxes culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kura%E2%80%93Araxes_culture) (circa 3400 BC—ca. 2000 BC) stretched from northwestern Iran up into the neighbouring regions of the [Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus) and [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia).[^cite_note-35][^cite_note-36] Susa is one of the oldest-known settlements of Iran and the world. The general perception among archaeologists is that Susa was an extension of the [Sumerian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumer) city-state of [Uruk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uruk), hence incorporating many aspects of Mesopotamian culture.[^cite_note-37][^cite_note-38] In its later history, Susa became the capital of Elam, which emerged as a state founded 4000 BC.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPotts1999\[httpsarchiveorgdetailsarchaeologyofela0000pottpage45mode1upq4000_45–6\]-39] There are also dozens of prehistoric sites across the Iranian plateau pointing to the existence of ancient cultures and urban settlements in the fourth millennium BC.[^cite_note-xinhuaNet_10082007-31] One of the earliest civilizations on the Iranian plateau was the [Jiroft culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jiroft_culture) in southeastern Iran in the province of [Kerman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kerman).

Iran is one of the most artefact-rich archaeological sites in [West Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Asia). Archaeological excavations in [Jiroft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jiroft) led to the discovery of several objects belonging to the 4th millennium BC.[^cite_note-40] There is a large quantity of objects decorated with highly distinctive engravings of animals, mythological figures, and architectural motifs. The objects and their iconography are considered unique. Many are made from [chlorite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chlorite_group), a grey-green soft stone; others are in [copper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copper), [bronze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze), [terracotta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terracotta), and even [lapis lazuli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lapis_lazuli). Recent excavations at the sites have produced the world's earliest inscription which pre-dates Mesopotamian inscriptions.[^cite_note-41][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIranica_Online_-_Iranian_History_(1)_Pre-Islamic_Times-42]

There are records of numerous other ancient civilizations on the Iranian plateau before the emergence of [Iranian peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_peoples) during the [Early Iron Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Iron_Age). The [Early Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Bronze_Age) saw the rise of urbanization into organized city-states and the invention of writing (the [Uruk period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uruk_period)) in the Near East. While Bronze Age Elam made use of writing from an early time, the [Proto-Elamite script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Elamite#Proto-Elamite_script) remains undeciphered, and records from Sumer pertaining to Elam are scarce.

Russian historian [Igor M. Diakonoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igor_M._Diakonoff) states that the modern inhabitants of Iran are descendants of mainly non-Indo-European groups, more specifically of pre-Iranic inhabitants of the Iranian Plateau.[^cite_note-44]

### Early Iron Age

Further information: [Persis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persis) and [Fars territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fars_territory)

See also: [Fars province](448049.md) and [Persian people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_people)



[Rhyton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhyton) in the shape of a ram's head, gold – [Saqqez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saqqez) - [Kurdistan](61733686.md) - western Iran

[^cite_note-EIO_ziwiye-5]

&#x20;–, late 7th–early 6th century BCE



A gold cup at the [National Museum of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Iran), from the first half of the 1st millennium BC

Records become more tangible with the rise of the [Neo-Assyrian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Assyrian_Empire) and its records of incursions from the Iranian plateau. As early as the 20th century BC, tribes came to the Iranian plateau from the [Pontic–Caspian steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontic%E2%80%93Caspian_steppe). The arrival of the [Iranic peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranic_peoples) on the Iranian plateau forced the Elamites to relinquish one area of their empire after another and to take refuge in Elam, [Khuzestan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Khuzestan_province) and the nearby area, which only then became coterminous with Elam.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIranica_Online_-_Elam_vii.\[httpswwwiranicaonlineorgarticleselam-vii_Non-Elamite_texts_in_Elam\]-45] Bahman Firuzmandi says that the southern Iranians might be intermixed with the Elamite peoples living in the plateau.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESarfarazFiruzmandi199620-46] By the mid-1st millennium BC, [Medes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medes), [Persians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_people), and [Parthians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parthia) populated the Iranian plateau. Until the rise of the Medes, they all remained under [Assyrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyria) domination, like the rest of the [Near East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near_East). In the first half of the 1st millennium BC, parts of what is now [Iranian Azerbaijan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijan_\(Iran\)) were incorporated into [Urartu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urartu).

## &#x20;Classical antiquity

### Median and Achaemenid Empires (678–330 BC)

Main articles: [Median kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median_kingdom) and [Achaemenid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire)

See also: [Greco-Persian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Persian_Wars)

- The tomb of Cyrus the Great

  The tomb of [Cyrus the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrus_the_Great)

- Ruins of the Gate of All Nations, Persepolis

  Ruins of the [Gate of All Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gate_of_All_Nations), Persepolis

- Ruins of the Apadana, Persepolis

  Ruins of the [Apadana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apadana), Persepolis

- Depiction of united Medes and Persians at the Apadana, Persepolis

  Depiction of united [Medes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medes) and [Persians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_people) at the [Apadana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apadana), Persepolis

- Ruins of the Tachara, Persepolis

  Ruins of the [Tachara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tachara), Persepolis

In 646 BC, [Assyrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyria) king [Ashurbanipal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashurbanipal) sacked [Susa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susa), which ended Elamite supremacy in the region.[^cite_note-MMA_IranTLine_2-47] For over 150 years Assyrian kings of nearby northern [Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia) had been wanting to conquer Median tribes of western Iran.[^cite_note-48] Under pressure from Assyria, the small kingdoms of the western Iranian plateau coalesced into increasingly larger and more centralized states.[^cite_note-MMA_IranTLine_2-47]



The Medes at the time of their maximum expansion

In the second half of the 7th century BC, the Medes gained their independence and were united by [Deioces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deioces). In 612 BC, [Cyaxares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyaxares), Deioces' grandson, and the [Babylonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylonia) king [Nabopolassar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nabopolassar) invaded Assyria and laid siege to and eventually destroyed [Nineveh](21699.md), the Assyrian capital, which led to the fall of the [Neo-Assyrian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Assyrian_Empire).[^cite_note-49] Urartu was later on conquered and dissolved as well by the Medes.[^cite_note-50][^cite_note-51] The Medes are credited with founding Iran as a nation and empire, and established the first Iranian empire, the largest of its day until [Cyrus the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrus_the_Great) established a unified empire of the Medes and Persians, leading to the Achaemenid Empire (c. 550–330 BC).



The Achaemenid Empire at its greatest extent

Cyrus the Great overthrew, in turn, the Median, [Lydian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lydia), and [Neo-Babylonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Babylonian_Empire) empires, creating an empire far larger than Assyria. He was better able, through more benign policies, to reconcile his subjects to Persian rule; the longevity of his empire was one result. The Persian king, like the Assyrian, was also "[King of Kings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Kings)", *xšāyaθiya xšāyaθiyānām* (*shāhanshāh* in modern Persian) – "great king", [Megas Basileus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basileus), as known by the [Greeks](42056.md).

Cyrus's son, [Cambyses II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambyses_II), conquered the last major power of the region, [ancient Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egypt), causing the collapse of the [Twenty-sixth Dynasty of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twenty-sixth_Dynasty_of_Egypt). Since he became ill and died before, or while, leaving Egypt, stories developed, as related by [Herodotus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herodotus), that he was struck down for impiety against the [ancient Egyptian deities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egyptian_deities). After the death of Cambyses II, [Darius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darius_the_Great) ascended the throne by overthrowing the legitimate Achaemenid monarch [Bardiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bardiya), and then quelling rebellions throughout his kingdom. As the winner, Darius based his claim on membership in a collateral line of the Achaemenid Empire.

Darius' first capital was at Susa, and he started the building program at [Persepolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persepolis). He rebuilt a canal between the [Nile](21244.md) and the [Red Sea](26191.md), a forerunner of the modern [Suez Canal](29323.md). He improved the extensive road system, and it is during his reign that mentions are first made of the [Royal Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Road) (shown on map), a great highway stretching all the way from Susa to [Sardis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sardis) with posting stations at regular intervals. Major reforms took place under Darius. Coinage, in the form of the *[daric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daric)* (gold coin) and the [shekel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shekel) (silver coin), was standardized (coinage had been invented over a century before in Lydia c. 660 BC but not standardized),[^cite_note-52] and administrative efficiency increased.

The [Old Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Persian) language appears in royal inscriptions, written in a specially adapted version of the [cuneiform script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuneiform_script). Under Cyrus the Great and Darius, the Persian Empire eventually became the largest empire in human history up until that point, ruling and administrating over most of the known world,[^cite_note-53] as well as spanning the continents of Europe, Asia, and Africa. The greatest achievement was the empire itself. The Persian Empire represented the world's first [superpower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superpower)[^cite_note-54] that was based on a model of tolerance and respect for other cultures and religions.[^cite_note-55]



Map showing key sites during the Persian invasions of Greece.

In the late 6th century BC, Darius launched his European campaign, in which he defeated the [Paeonians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paeonia_\(kingdom\)), conquered [Thrace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thrace), and subdued all coastal Greek cities, [as well as defeating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Scythian_campaign_of_Darius_I) the European [Scythians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scythians) around the [Danube](21209639.md) river.[^cite_note-RoisWort2010_345-56] In 512/511 BC, [Macedon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonia_\(ancient_kingdom\)) became a [vassal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vassal) kingdom of Persia.[^cite_note-RoisWort2010_345-56]

In 499 BC, [Athens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Athens) lent support to a revolt in [Miletus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miletus), which resulted in the sacking of [Sardis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sardis). This led to an Achaemenid campaign against mainland Greece known as the [Greco-Persian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Persian_Wars), which lasted the first half of the 5th century BC, and is known as one of the most important wars in European history. In the [First Persian invasion of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Persian_invasion_of_Greece), the Persian general [Mardonius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mardonius_\(nephew_of_Darius_I\)) re-subjugated Thrace and made Macedon a full part of Persia.[^cite_note-RoisWort2010_345-56] The war eventually turned out in defeat, however. Darius' successor [Xerxes I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xerxes_I) launched the [Second Persian invasion of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Persian_invasion_of_Greece). At a crucial moment in the war, about half of mainland Greece was overrun by the Persians, including all territories to the north of the [Isthmus of Corinth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isthmus_of_Corinth),[^cite_note-57][^cite_note-58] however, this was also turned out in a Greek victory, following the battles of [Plataea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Plataea) and [Salamis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Salamis), by which Persia lost its footholds in Europe, and eventually withdrew from it.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERoismanWorthington2010\[httpsbooksgooglecabooksidQsJ183uUDkMCpgPA135_135–8\],_342–5-59] During the Greco-Persian wars, the Persians gained major territorial advantages. They [captured and razed Athens twice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_destruction_of_Athens), once in 480 BC and again in 479 BC. However, after a string of Greek victories the Persians were forced to withdraw, thus losing control of [Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonia_\(ancient_kingdom\)), [Thrace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thrace) and [Ionia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionia). Fighting continued for several decades after the successful Greek repelling of the Second Invasion with numerous Greek city-states under the Athens' newly formed [Delian League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delian_League), which eventually ended with the [peace of Callias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_of_Callias) in 449 BC, ending the Greco-Persian Wars. In 404 BC, following the death of [Darius II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darius_II), Egypt rebelled under [Amyrtaeus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amyrtaeus). Later [pharaohs](23294.md) successfully resisted Persian attempts to reconquer Egypt until 343 BC, when Egypt was reconquered by [Artaxerxes III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artaxerxes_III).



A panoramic view of [Persepolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persepolis)

### Greek conquest (331 BC) and Seleucid Empire (312 BC–248 BC)



The [Seleucid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucid_Empire) in 200 BC, before Antiochus was defeated by the Romans

From 334 BC to 331 BC, [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great) defeated [Darius III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darius_III) in the battles of [Granicus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Granicus), [Issus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Issus) and [Gaugamela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Gaugamela), and conquered the Achaemenid Empire by 331 BC. Alexander's empire broke up shortly after his death, and Alexander's general, [Seleucus I Nicator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucus_I_Nicator), tried to take control of Iran, [Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia), and later [Syria](7515849.md) and [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia). His empire was the [Seleucid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucid_Empire). He was killed in 281 BC by [Ptolemy Keraunos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptolemy_Keraunos).

### Parthian Empire (248 BC–224 AD)

See also: [Roman–Parthian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman%E2%80%93Parthian_Wars)



[Bagadates I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bagadates_I), first native Persian ruler after Greek rule

The [Parthian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parthian_Empire)—ruled by the Parthians, a group of northwestern Iranian people—was the realm of the Arsacid dynasty. This latter reunited and governed the Iranian plateau after the [Parni conquest of Parthia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parni_conquest_of_Parthia) and defeating the Seleucid Empire in the late 3rd century BC. It intermittently controlled Mesopotamia between c. 150 BC and 224 AD and absorbed [Eastern Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Arabia).

Parthia was the eastern arch-enemy of the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire), and it limited Rome's expansion beyond [Cappadocia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cappadocia) (central Anatolia). The Parthian armies included two types of [cavalry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cavalry): the heavily armed and armored [cataphracts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cataphract) and the lightly armed but highly-mobile [mounted archers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mounted_archery).

For the Romans, who relied on heavy [infantry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infantry), the Parthians were too hard to defeat, as both types of cavalry were much faster and more mobile than foot soldiers. The [Parthian shot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parthian_shot) used by the Parthian cavalry was most notably feared by the Roman soldiers, which proved pivotal in the crushing Roman defeat at the [Battle of Carrhae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Carrhae). On the other hand, the Parthians found it difficult to occupy conquered areas as they were unskilled in [siege](26905.md) warfare. Because of these weaknesses, neither the Romans nor the Parthians were able completely to [annex](18950961.md) each other's territory.

The Parthian empire subsisted for five centuries, longer than most Eastern Empires. The end of this empire came at last in 224 AD, when the empire's organization had loosened and the last king was defeated by one of the empire's vassal peoples, the Persians under the Sasanians. However, the Arsacid dynasty continued to exist for centuries onwards in [Armenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsacid_dynasty_of_Armenia), the [Iberia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsacid_dynasty_of_Iberia), and the [Caucasian Albania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsacid_Dynasty_of_Caucasian_Albania), which were all eponymous branches of the dynasty.

### Sasanian Empire (224–651 AD)

See also: [Roman–Iranian relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman%E2%80%93Iranian_relations), [Byzantine–Sasanian wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine%E2%80%93Sasanian_wars), and [Byzantine–Sasanian War of 602–628](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine%E2%80%93Sasanian_War_of_602%E2%80%93628)



Rock-face relief at [Naqsh-e Rustam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naqsh-e_Rustam) of Iranian emperor [Shapur I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shapur_I) (on horseback) capturing Roman emperor [Valerian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valerian_\(emperor\)) (kneeing) and [Philip the Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_the_Arab) (standing).



Hunting scene on a [gilded silver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilded_silver) bowl showing king [Khosrau I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khosrau_I).

The first shah of the [Sasanian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sasanian_Empire), [Ardashir I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ardashir_I), started reforming the country economically and militarily. For a period of more than 400 years, Iran was once again one of the leading powers in the world, alongside its neighbouring rival, the [Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) and then [Byzantine Empires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire).[^cite_note-60][^cite_note-61] The empire's territory, at its height, encompassed all of today's Iran, [Iraq](7515928.md), [Azerbaijan](746.md), [Armenia](10918072.md), [Georgia](48768.md), [Abkhazia](18933375.md), [Dagestan](751099.md), [Lebanon](17771.md), [Jordan](7515964.md), [Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_\(region\)), [Israel](9282173.md), parts of [Afghanistan](737.md), [Turkey](11125639.md), [Syria](7515849.md), parts of [Pakistan](23235.md), [Central Asia](6742.md), [Eastern Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Arabia), and parts of [Egypt](8087628.md).

Most of the Sasanian Empire's lifespan was overshadowed by the frequent [Byzantine–Sasanian wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine%E2%80%93Sasanian_wars), a continuation of the [Roman–Parthian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman%E2%80%93Parthian_Wars) and the all-comprising [Roman–Persian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman%E2%80%93Persian_Wars); the last was the longest-lasting conflict in human history. Started in the first century BC by their predecessors, the Parthians, and Romans, the last Roman–Persian War was fought in the seventh century. The Persians defeated the Romans at the [Battle of Edessa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Edessa) in 260 and took emperor [Valerian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valerian_\(emperor\)) prisoner for the remainder of his life. Eastern Arabia was conquered early on. During [Khosrow II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khosrow_II)'s rule in 590–628, [Egypt](8087628.md), [Jordan](7515964.md), [Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_\(region\)) and [Lebanon](17771.md) were also annexed to the Empire. The Sassanians called their empire *Erânshahr* ("Dominion of the Aryans", i.e., of [Iranians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_peoples)).[^cite_note-62]

A chapter of Iran's history followed after roughly 600 years of conflict with the Roman Empire. During this time, the Sassanian and Romano-Byzantine armies clashed for influence in Anatolia, the western Caucasus (mainly [Lazica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lazica) and the [Kingdom of Iberia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Iberia); modern-day [Georgia](48768.md) and [Abkhazia](18933375.md)), [Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia), Armenia and the Levant. Under Justinian I, the war came to an uneasy peace with payment of tribute to the Sassanians. However, the Sasanians used the deposition of the Byzantine emperor [Maurice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_\(emperor\)) as a *casus belli* to attack the Empire. After many gains, the Sassanians were defeated at Issus, Constantinople, and finally Nineveh, resulting in peace. With the conclusion of the over 700 years lasting Roman–Persian Wars through the climactic [Byzantine–Sasanian War of 602–628](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine%E2%80%93Sasanian_War_of_602%E2%80%93628), which included the very [siege of the Byzantine capital of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Constantinople_\(626\)), the war-exhausted Persians lost the [Battle of al-Qādisiyyah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_al-Q%C4%81disiyyah) (632) in [Hilla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hilla) (present-day [Iraq](7515928.md)) to the invading Muslim forces.

The Sasanian era, encompassing the length of [Late Antiquity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Antiquity), is considered to be one of the most important and influential historical periods in Iran, and had a major impact on the world. In many ways, the Sassanian period witnessed the highest achievement of Persian civilization and constitutes the last great Iranian Empire before the adoption of Islam. Persia influenced Roman civilization considerably during Sassanian times,[^cite_note-63] their cultural influence extending far beyond the empire's territorial borders, reaching as far as Western Europe,[^cite_note-autogenerated2-64] Africa,[^cite_note-65] China and India[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESarfarazFiruzmandi1996329–30-66] and also playing a prominent role in the formation of both European and Asiatic medieval art.[^cite_note-67]

This influence carried forward to the [Muslim world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_world). The dynasty's unique and aristocratic culture transformed the Islamic conquest and destruction of Iran into a Persian Renaissance.[^cite_note-autogenerated2-64] Much of what later became known as Islamic culture, architecture, writing, and other contributions to civilization, were taken from the Sassanian Persians into the broader Muslim world.[^cite_note-68]



Battle between [Heraclius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heraclius)' army and Persians under [Khosrow II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khosrow_II). Fresco by [Piero della Francesca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piero_della_Francesca), c. 1452.

## Medieval period

### Early Islamic period

See also: [Islamization of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamization_of_Iran)

#### Islamic conquest of Persia (633–651)

Main article: [Muslim conquest of Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_Persia)

See also: [Persian revolts against the Rashidun Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_revolts_against_the_Rashidun_Caliphate)



Phases of the Islamic conquest&#x20;

&#x20; Expansion under the Prophet Muhammad, 622–632

&#x20; Expansion during the Rashidun Caliphate, 632–661

&#x20; Expansion during the Umayyad Caliphate, 661–750

In 633, immediately following [a civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sasanian_civil_war_of_628%E2%80%93632) and during the reign of the Sasanian king [Yazdegerd III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yazdegerd_III), the Muslims under [Umar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umar) invaded Iran. Several Iranian nobles and families such as king Dinar of the [House of Karen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Karen), and later [Kanarangiyans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanarang) of [Khorasan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khorasan), mutinied against their Sasanian overlords. Although the [House of Mihran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Mihran) had claimed the Sasanian throne under the two prominent generals [Bahram Chobin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahram_Chobin) and [Shahrbaraz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahrbaraz), it remained loyal to the Sasanians during its struggle against the Arabs, but the Mihrans were eventually betrayed and defeated by their own kinsmen, the [House of Ispahbudhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Ispahbudhan), under their leader [Farrukhzad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farrukhzad), who had mutinied against Yazdegerd III. Yazdegerd III fled from one district to another until a local miller killed him for his purse at [Merv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merv) in 651.[^cite_note-Brit_Iran-17] By 674, Muslims had conquered [Khorasan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khorasan) (which included [Khorasan province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khorasan_province) and modern Afghanistan and parts of [Transoxiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transoxiana)).

The Muslim conquest of Persia ended the Sasanian Empire and led to the eventual decline of the [Zoroastrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoroastrianism) religion in Persia. Over time, the majority of Iranians converted to Islam. Most of the aspects of the previous Persian civilizations were not discarded but were absorbed by the new Islamic polity. As [Bernard Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernard_Lewis) has commented:

> These events have been variously seen in Iran: by some as a blessing, the advent of the true faith, the end of the age of ignorance and heathenism; by others as a humiliating national defeat, the conquest and subjugation of the country by foreign invaders. Both perceptions are of course valid, depending on one's angle of vision.[^cite_note-lewis-69]

#### Umayyad era and Muslim incursions into the Caspian coast

After the fall of the Sasanian Empire in 651, the [Arabs](2185.md) of the [Umayyad Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate) adopted many Persian customs, especially the administrative and the court mannerisms. Arab provincial governors were undoubtedly either Persianized [Arameans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arameans) or ethnic Persians; certainly Persian remained the language of official business of the caliphate until the adoption of Arabic toward the end of the 7th century,[^cite_note-70] when in 692 minting began at the capital [Damascus](8914.md). The Islamic coins evolved from imitations of Sasanian coins (as well as [Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine)), and the [Pahlavi script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pahlavi_script) on the coinage was replaced with [Arabic alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_alphabet).

During the Umayyad Caliphate, the Arab conquerors imposed [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) as the primary language of the subject peoples throughout their empire. [Al-Hajjaj ibn Yusuf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Hajjaj_ibn_Yusuf), who was not happy with the prevalence of the Persian language in the [divan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divan), ordered the official language of the conquered lands to be replaced by Arabic, sometimes by force.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Cambridge_History_of_Iran_Vol._4p._\[httpsarchiveorgdetailsZarrinkub1975ArabConquestCHI04pagen48mode1up_46\]-71] In [al-Biruni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Biruni)'s *From the Remaining Signs of Past Centuries* for example it is written:

> When [Qutaibah bin Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qutayba_ibn_Muslim) under the command of Al-Hajjaj bin Yousef was sent to [Khwarazmia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khwarezm) with a military expedition and conquered it for the second time, he swiftly killed whoever wrote the Khwarazmian native language that knew of the Khwarazmian heritage, history, and culture. He then killed all their Zoroastrian priests and burned and wasted their books, until gradually the illiterate only remained, who knew nothing of writing, and hence their history was mostly forgotten.[^cite_note-72]

Several historians see the rule of the Umayyads as setting up the "dhimmah" to increase taxes from the *[dhimmis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhimmi)* to benefit the Muslim Arab community financially and by discouraging conversion.[^cite_note-Astren2004_33-73] Governors lodged complaints with the caliph when he enacted laws that made conversion easier, depriving the provinces of revenues. In the 7th century, when many non-Arabs such as Persians entered Islam, they were recognized as [mawali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mawla) ("clients") and treated as second-class citizens by the ruling Arab elite until the end of the Umayyad Caliphate. During this era, Islam was initially associated with the ethnic identity of the Arab and required formal association with an [Arab tribe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribes_of_Arabia) and the adoption of the client status of *mawali*.[^cite_note-Astren2004_33-73] The half-hearted policies of the late Umayyads to tolerate non-Arab Muslims and Shias had failed to quell unrest among these minorities.

However, all of Iran was still not under Arab control; the region of [Daylam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daylam) was under the control of the [Daylamites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daylamites), while [Tabaristan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabaristan) was under [Dabuyid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dabuyid_dynasty) and [Paduspanid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paduspanids) control, and the [Mount Damavand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Damavand) region was under [Masmughans of Damavand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masmughans_of_Damavand). The Arabs had invaded these regions several times but achieved no decisive result because of the inaccessible terrain of the regions. The most prominent ruler of the Dabuyids, known as [Farrukhan the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farrukhan_the_Great) (r. 712–728), managed to hold his domains during his long struggle against the Arab general [Yazid ibn al-Muhallab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yazid_ibn_al-Muhallab), who was defeated by a combined Dailamite-Dabuyid army and was forced to retreat from Tabaristan.[^cite_note-74]

With the death of the Umayyad Caliph [Hisham ibn Abd al-Malik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hisham_ibn_Abd_al-Malik) in 743, the Islamic world was launched into civil war. [Abu Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Muslim) was sent to Khorasan by the [Abbasid Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid_Caliphate) initially as a propagandist and then to revolt on their behalf. He took [Merv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merv) defeating the Umayyad governor [Nasr ibn Sayyar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasr_ibn_Sayyar). He became the de facto Abbasid governor of Khurasan. During the same period, the Dabuyid ruler [Khurshid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khurshid_of_Tabaristan) declared independence from the Umayyads but was shortly forced to recognize Abbasid authority. In 750, Abu Muslim became the leader of the Abbasid army and defeated the Umayyads at the [Battle of the Zab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Zab). Abu Muslim stormed Damascus later that year.

#### Abbasid period and autonomous Iranian dynasties

See also: [Iranian Intermezzo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Intermezzo)



The [Saffarid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saffarid_dynasty) in 900 AD.



Map of the Iranian dynasties in the mid 10th-century.

The Abbasid army consisted primarily of Khorasanians and was led by Abu Muslim. It contained both Iranian and Arab elements, and the Abbasids enjoyed both Iranian and Arab support. The Abbasids overthrew the Umayyads in 750.[^cite_note-ICS_IslamicConquest-75] According to Amir Arjomand, the [Abbasid Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid_Revolution) essentially marked the end of the Arab empire and the beginning of a more inclusive, multi-ethnic state in the Middle East.[^cite_note-76] One of the first changes the Abbasids made after taking power from the Umayyads was to move the empire's capital to Iraq. The latter region was influenced by Persian history and culture, and moving the capital was part of the Persian mawali demand for Arab influence in the empire. The city of [Baghdad](4492.md) was constructed on the [Tigris River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tigris_River), in 762, to serve as the Abbasid capital.[^cite_note-AHGC_UnCalgary-77]

The Abbasids established the position of [vizier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vizier) like [Barmakids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barmakids) in their administration, which was the equivalent of a "vice-caliph", or second-in-command. Eventually, this change meant that many caliphs under the Abbasids ended up in a much more ceremonial role than ever before, with the vizier in real power. A new Persian bureaucracy began to replace the old Arab aristocracy, and the entire administration reflected these changes, demonstrating that the new dynasty was different in many ways from the Umayyads.[^cite_note-AHGC_UnCalgary-77]

By the 9th century, Abbasid control began to wane as regional leaders sprang up in the far corners of the empire to challenge the central authority of the Abbasid caliphate.[^cite_note-AHGC_UnCalgary-77] The Abbasid caliphs began enlisting *[mamluks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamluk)*, Turkic-speaking warriors, who had been moving out of Central Asia into Transoxiana as slave warriors as early as the 9th century. Shortly thereafter the real power of the Abbasid caliphs began to wane; eventually, they became religious figureheads while the warrior slaves ruled.[^cite_note-ICS_IslamicConquest-75]

The 9th century also saw the revolt by native Zoroastrians, known as the [Khurramites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khurramites), against oppressive Arab rule. The movement was led by a Persian freedom fighter [Babak Khorramdin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babak_Khorramdin). Babak's Iranianizing rebellion,[^cite_note-79] from its base in Azerbaijan in northwestern Iran,[^cite_note-81] called for a return of the political glories of the Iranian[^cite_note-83] past. The Khorramdin rebellion of Babak spread to the western and central parts of Iran and lasted more than 20 years before it was defeated when Babak was betrayed by [Afshin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afshin_\(Caliphate_General\)), a senior general of the Abbasid Caliphate.

As the power of the Abbasid caliphs diminished, a series of dynasties rose in various parts of Iran, some with considerable influence and power. Among the most important of these overlapping dynasties were the [Tahirids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahirid_dynasty) in Khorasan (821–873); the [Saffarids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saffarid_dynasty) in [Sistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sistan) (861–1003, their rule lasted as maliks of Sistan until 1537); and the [Samanids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samanid_Empire) (819–1005), originally at [Bukhara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bukhara). The Samanids eventually ruled an area from central Iran to Pakistan.[^cite_note-ICS_IslamicConquest-75]

By the early 10th century, the Abbasids almost lost control to the growing Persian faction known as the [Buyid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buyid_dynasty) (934–1062). Since much of the Abbasid administration had been Persian anyway, the Buyids were quietly able to assume real power in Baghdad. The Buyids were defeated in the mid-11th century by the [Seljuq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seljuk_Empire) [Turks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oghuz_Turks), who continued to exert influence over the Abbasids, while publicly pledging allegiance to them. The balance of power in Baghdad remained as such – with the Abbasids in power in name only – until the Mongol invasion of 1258 sacked the city and definitively ended the Abbasid dynasty.[^cite_note-AHGC_UnCalgary-77]

During the [Abbasid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid) period an enfranchisement was experienced by the *mawali* and a shift was made in political conception from that of a primarily Arab empire to one of a Muslim empire[^cite_note-Tobin-84] and c. 930 a requirement was enacted for all bureaucrats of the empire to be Muslim.[^cite_note-Astren2004_33-73]

#### Islamic golden age, Shu'ubiyya movement and Persianization process



Extract from a medieval manuscript by [Qotbeddin Shirazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qutb_al-Din_al-Shirazi) (1236–1311), a Persian astronomer, depicting an epicyclic planetary model

[Islamization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamization) was a long process by which Islam was gradually adopted by the majority population of Iran. [Richard Bulliet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Bulliet)'s "conversion curve" indicates that only about 10% of Iran converted to Islam during the relatively Arab-centric Umayyad period. Beginning in the Abbasid period, with its mix of Persian as well as Arab rulers, the Muslim percentage of the population rose. As Persian Muslims consolidated their rule of the country, the Muslim population rose from approximately 40% in the mid-9th century to close to 90% by the end of the 11th century.[^cite_note-Tobin-84] [Seyyed Hossein Nasr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seyyed_Hossein_Nasr) suggests that the rapid increase in conversion was aided by the Persian nationality of the rulers.[^cite_note-85] Although Persians adopted the religion of their conquerors, over the centuries they worked to protect and revive their distinctive language and culture, a process known as [Persianization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persianization). Arabs and Turks participated in this attempt.[^cite_note-fn_Brit_Seljuq-87][^cite_note-88][^cite_note-89]

In the 9th and 10th centuries, non-Arab subjects of the [Ummah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ummah) created a movement called [Shu'ubiyyah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shu'ubiyyah) in response to the privileged status of Arabs. Most of those behind the movement were Persian, but references to [Egyptians](31912046.md), [Berbers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berber_people) and [Aramaeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aramaeans) are attested.[^cite_note-90] Citing as its basis Islamic notions of equality of races and nations, the movement was primarily concerned with preserving Persian culture and protecting Persian identity, though within a Muslim context.

The Samanid dynasty led the revival of Persian culture and the first important Persian poet after the arrival of Islam, [Rudaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudaki), was born during this era and was praised by Samanid kings. The Samanids also revived many ancient Persian festivals. Their successor, the [Ghaznawids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghaznavids), who were of non-Iranian Turkic origin, also became instrumental in the revival of Persian culture.[^cite_note-91]



Persian manuscript describing how an ambassador from India, probably sent by the [Maukhari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maukhari) King [Śarvavarman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Aarvavarman) of [Kannauj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kannauj), brought [chess](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shatranj) to the Persian court of [Khosrow I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khosrow_I).

[^cite_note-92]

[^cite_note-93]

The culmination of the Persianization movement was the *[Shahnameh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahnameh)*, the national epic of Iran, written almost entirely in Persian. This voluminous work, reflects Iran's ancient history, its unique cultural values, its pre-Islamic Zoroastrian religion, and its sense of nationhood. According to [Bernard Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernard_Lewis):[^cite_note-lewis-69]

> Iran was indeed Islamized, but it was not Arabized. Persians remained Persians. And after an interval of silence, Iran re-emerged as a separate, different and distinctive element within Islam, eventually adding a new element even to Islam itself. Culturally, politically, and most remarkable of all even religiously, the Iranian contribution to this new Islamic civilization is of immense importance. The work of Iranians can be seen in every field of cultural endeavour, including Arabic poetry, to which poets of Iranian origin composing their poems in Arabic made a very significant contribution. In a sense, Iranian Islam is a second advent of Islam itself, a new Islam sometimes referred to as Islam-i Ajam. It was this Persian Islam, rather than the original Arab Islam, that was brought to new areas and new peoples: to the Turks, first in Central Asia and then in the Middle East in the country which came to be called Turkey, and of course to India. The Ottoman Turks brought a form of Iranian civilization to the walls of Vienna...

The [Islamization of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamization_of_Iran) was to yield deep transformations within the cultural, scientific, and political structure of Iran's society: The blossoming of [Persian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_literature), [philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_philosophy), [medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_and_technology_in_Iran) and [art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_art) became major elements of the newly forming Muslim civilization. Inheriting a heritage of thousands of years of civilization, and being at the "crossroads of the major cultural highways",[^cite_note-94] contributed to Persia emerging as what culminated into the "[Islamic Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Golden_Age)". During this period, [hundreds of scholars and scientists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Iranian_scientists_and_scholars) vastly contributed to technology, science and medicine, later influencing the rise of European science during [the Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Renaissance).[^cite_note-95]

The most important scholars of almost all of the Islamic sects and schools of thought were Persian or lived in Iran, including the most notable and reliable [Hadith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith) collectors of [Shia](26961.md) and [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni) like [Shaikh Saduq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaikh_Saduq), [Shaikh Kulainy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Ya'qub_Kulainy), [Hakim al-Nishaburi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hakim_al-Nishaburi), [Imam Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_ibn_al-Hajjaj) and Imam Bukhari, the greatest [theologians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalam) of Shia and Sunni like [Shaykh Tusi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaykh_Tusi), [Imam Ghazali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Ghazali), [Imam Fakhr al-Razi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fakhr_al-Din_al-Razi) and [Al-Zamakhshari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Zamakhshari), the greatest [physicians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physicians), [astronomers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_astronomy), [logicians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logic_in_Islamic_philosophy), [mathematicians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_mathematics), [metaphysicians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metaphysics), [philosophers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Islamic_philosophy) and [scientists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_science) like [Avicenna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avicenna) and [Nasīr al-Dīn al-Tūsī](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nas%C4%ABr_al-D%C4%ABn_al-T%C5%ABs%C4%AB), and the greatest [shaykhs of Sufism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikh_\(Sufism\)) like [Rumi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jalal_ad-Din_Muhammad_Rumi) and [Abdul-Qadir Gilani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Qadir_Gilani).

#### Persianate states and dynasties (977–1219)

Main article: [Ghaznavid campaigns in Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghaznavid_campaigns_in_Persia)



The [Kharraqan Towers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kharraqan_Towers), built in 1067, Iran, contain tombs of Seljuq princes.

In 977, a Turkic governor of the Samanids, [Sabuktigin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabuktigin), conquered [Ghazna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazna) (in present-day Afghanistan) and established a dynasty, the [Ghaznavids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghaznavids), that lasted to 1186.[^cite_note-ICS_IslamicConquest-75] The Ghaznavid empire grew by taking all of the Samanid territories south of the [Amu Darya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amu_Darya) in the last decade of the 10th century, and eventually occupied parts of Eastern Iran, Afghanistan, Pakistan and north-west India.[^cite_note-AHGC_UnCalgary-77] The Ghaznavids are generally credited with launching Islam into a mainly [Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu) India. The invasion of India was undertaken in 1000 by the Ghaznavid ruler [Mahmud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmud_of_Ghazni) and continued for several years. They were unable to hold power for long, however, particularly after the death of Mahmud in 1030. By 1040, the [Seljuqs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seljuk_dynasty) had taken over the Ghaznavid lands in Iran.[^cite_note-AHGC_UnCalgary-77]

The Seljuqs, who like the Ghaznavids were Persianate in nature and of Turkic origin, slowly conquered Iran over the course of the 11th century.[^cite_note-ICS_IslamicConquest-75] The dynasty had its origins in the [Turcoman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkoman_\(ethnonym\)) tribal confederations of Central Asia and marked the beginning of [Turkic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_peoples) power in West Asia. They established a [Sunni Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Muslim) rule over parts of Central and West Asia from the 11th to 14th centuries. They set the [Seljuq Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seljuk_Empire) that stretched from Anatolia in the west to western Afghanistan in the east and the western borders of modern-day China in the north-east; and was the target of the [First Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Crusade). Today they are regarded as the cultural ancestors of the Western Turks, the present-day inhabitants of Turkey, Azerbaijan and Turkmenistan, and they are remembered as great patrons of [Persian culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_culture), [art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_art), [literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_literature), and [language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_language).[^cite_note-fn_Brit_Seljuq-87][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIranica_Online_-_Persian_Manuscripts_I.\[httpswwwiranicaonlineorgarticlespersian-manuscripts-1-ottoman_In_Ottoman_And_modern_Turkish_Libraries\]-96][^cite_note-97]



Seljuq empire at the time of its greatest extent, at the death of Malik Shah I

The founder of the dynasty, [Tughril Beg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tughril_Beg), turned his army against the Ghaznavids in Khorasan. He moved south and then west, conquering but not wasting the cities in his path. In 1055, the caliph in Baghdad gave Tughril Beg robes, gifts, and the title King of the East. Under Tughril Beg's successor, [Malik Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malik_Shah_I) (1072–1092), Iran enjoyed a cultural and scientific renaissance, largely attributed to his brilliant Iranian vizier, [Nizam al Mulk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nizam_al_Mulk). These leaders established the observatory where [Omar Khayyám](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omar_Khayy%C3%A1m) did much of his experimentation for a new calendar, and they built [religious schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nizamiyya) in all the major towns. They brought [Abu Hamid Ghazali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Ghazali), one of the greatest Islamic theologians, and other eminent scholars to the Seljuq capital at Baghdad and encouraged and supported their work.[^cite_note-ICS_IslamicConquest-75]

When Malik Shah I died in 1092, the empire split as his brother and four sons quarreled over the apportioning of the empire among themselves. In Anatolia, Malik Shah I was succeeded by [Kilij Arslan I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kilij_Arslan_I) who founded the [Sultanate of Rûm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_R%C3%BBm) and in Syria by his brother [Tutush I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tutush_I). In Iran he was succeeded by his son [Mahmud I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmud_I_of_Great_Seljuq) whose reign was contested by his other three brothers [Barkiyaruq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barkiyaruq) in Iraq, [Muhammad I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_I_\(Seljuq_sultan\)) in Baghdad and [Ahmad Sanjar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmed_Sanjar) in Khorasan. As Seljuq power in Iran weakened, other dynasties began to step up in its place, including a resurgent Abbasid caliphate and the [Khwarezmshahs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khwarazmian_Empire). The Khwarezmid Empire was a Sunni Muslim Persianate dynasty, of East Turkic origin, that ruled in Central Asia. Originally vassals of the Seljuqs, they took advantage of the decline of the Seljuqs to expand into Iran.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIranica_Online_-_Tekiš_B._Il_Arslān-98] In 1194, the Khwarezmshah [Ala ad-Din Tekish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ala_ad-Din_Tekish) defeated the Seljuq sultan [Toghrul III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toghrul_III_of_Seljuq) in battle and the Seljuq empire in Iran collapsed.[^cite_note-99] Of the former Seljuq Empire, only the Sultanate of Rum in Anatolia remained.

A serious internal threat to the Seljuqs during their reign came from the [Nizari Ismailis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nizari_Ismaili_state), a secret sect with headquarters at [Alamut Castle](205007.md) between [Rasht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rasht) and [Tehran](57654.md). They controlled the immediate area for more than 150 years and sporadically sent out adherents to strengthen their rule by murdering important officials. Several of the various theories on the etymology of the word *[assassin](2963.md)* derive from these killers.[^cite_note-ICS_IslamicConquest-75] Parts of northwestern Iran were conquered in the early 13th century AD by the [Kingdom of Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Georgia), led by [Tamar the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamar_the_Great).[^cite_note-100]

### Mongol conquest and rule (1219–1358)

#### Mongol invasion (1219–1221)

Main articles: [Mongol invasion of Central Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasion_of_Central_Asia), [Mongol invasion of Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasion_of_Persia), and [Mongol invasion of the Khwarazmian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasion_of_the_Khwarazmian_Empire)



[Eurasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasia) on the eve of the Mongol invasions, *c.* 1200



The Mongol Empire's expansion

The [Khwarazmian dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khwarazmian_dynasty) only lasted for a few decades, until the arrival of the [Mongols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongols). [Genghis Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genghis_Khan) had unified the Mongols, and under him the [Mongol Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_Empire) quickly expanded in several directions. In 1218, it bordered Khwarezm. At that time, the Khwarazmian Empire was ruled by [Ala ad-Din Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_II_of_Khwarezm) (1200–1220). Muhammad, like Genghis, was intent on expanding his lands and had gained the submission of most of Iran. He declared himself shah and demanded formal recognition from the Abbasid caliph [Al-Nasir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Nasir). When the caliph rejected his claim, Ala ad-Din Muhammad proclaimed one of his nobles caliph and unsuccessfully tried to depose an-Nasir.

The Mongol invasion of Iran began in 1219, after two diplomatic missions to Khwarezm sent by Genghis Khan had been massacred. During 1220–21 Bukhara, [Samarkand](51491.md), [Herat](14128.md), [Tus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tus,_Iran) and [Nishapur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nishapur) were razed, and the populations were slaughtered. The Khwarezm-Shah fled, to die on an island off the Caspian coast.[^cite_note-Brit_Iran-17] During the invasion of Transoxiana in 1219, along with the main Mongol force, Genghis Khan used a Chinese specialist catapult unit in battle; they were used again in 1220 in Transoxania. The Chinese may have used the catapults to hurl gunpowder bombs, since they already had them by this time.[^cite_note-102]

While Genghis Khan was conquering Transoxania and Iran, several Chinese who were familiar with gunpowder were serving in Genghis's army.[^cite_note-104] "Whole regiments" entirely made out of Chinese were used by the Mongols to command bomb hurling trebuchets during the invasion of Iran.[^cite_note-106] Historians have suggested that the Mongol invasion had brought Chinese gunpowder weapons to Central Asia. One of these was the [huochong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huochong), a Chinese mortar.[^cite_note-108] Books written around the area afterward depicted gunpowder weapons which resembled those of China.[^cite_note-109]

Before his death in 1227, Genghis had reached western Azerbaijan, pillaging and burning many cities along the way after entering Iran from the northeast. The Mongol invasion was by and large disastrous to the Iranians. Although the Mongol invaders eventually converted to Islam and accepted the culture of Iran, the Mongol destruction in Iran and other regions of the Islamic heartland (particularly the historical Khorasan region, mainly in Central Asia) marked a major change of direction for the region. Much of the six centuries of Islamic scholarship, culture, and infrastructure was destroyed as the invaders leveled cities, burned libraries, and in some cases replaced mosques with [Buddhist temples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_temple).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPow2022185-110][^cite_note-111][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIranica_Online_-_Iranian_History_(2)\[httpswwwiranicaonlineorgarticlesiran-ii2-islamic-period-page-3_Islamic_period_(page_3)\]-112] The Mongols killed many Iranian civilians. Destruction of [qanat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qanat) irrigation systems in the north east of Iran destroyed the pattern of relatively continuous settlements, producing many abandoned towns which were relatively quite good with irrigation and agriculture.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Cambridge_History_of_Iran_Vol._5-113] In 1221, Genghis Khan [destroyed the city of Gurganj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Gurganj). Most if not all the ancient [Iranic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranic) [Khwarazmian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khwarazmian_Empire) people were killed or pushed out, paving the way for the [Turkification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkification) of [Khwarazm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khwarazm).

#### Ilkhanate (1256–1335)



Mongol successor khanates

After Genghis's death, Iran was ruled by several Mongol commanders. Genghis' grandson, [Hulagu Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hulagu_Khan), was tasked with the westward expansion of Mongol dominion. However, by the time he ascended to power, the Mongol Empire had already dissolved, dividing into different factions. Arriving with an army, he established himself in the region and founded the [Ilkhanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilkhanate), a breakaway state of the Mongol Empire, which would rule Iran for the next 80 years and become Persian in the process.

Hulagu Khan seized Baghdad in 1258 and put the last Abbasid caliph to death. The westward advance of his forces was stopped by the [Mamelukes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahri_dynasty), however, at the [Battle of Ain Jalut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Ain_Jalut) in [Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_\(region\)) in 1260. Hulagu's campaigns against the Muslims also enraged [Berke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berke), khan of the [Golden Horde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Horde) and a convert to Islam. Hulagu and Berke fought against each other, demonstrating the weakening unity of the Mongol empire.

The rule of Hulagu's great-grandson, [Ghazan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazan) (1295–1304) saw the establishment of Islam as the state religion of the Ilkhanate. Ghazan and his famous Iranian vizier, [Rashid al-Din](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashid-al-Din_Hamadani), brought Iran a partial and brief economic revival. The Mongols lowered taxes for artisans, encouraged agriculture, rebuilt and extended irrigation works, and improved the safety of the trade routes. As a result, commerce increased dramatically.

Items from India, China, and Iran passed easily across the Asian steppes, and these contacts culturally enriched Iran. For example, Iranians developed a style of painting based on a unique fusion of solid, two-dimensional Mesopotamian painting with the feathery, light brush strokes and other motifs characteristic of China. After Ghazan's nephew [Abu Said](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Sa'id_Bahadur_Khan) died in 1335 the Ilkhanate lapsed into civil war and was divided between several petty dynasties – most prominently the [Jalayirids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jalayirids), [Muzaffarids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muzaffarids_\(Iran\)), [Sarbadars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarbadars) and [Kartids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kartids). The mid-14th-century [Black Death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Death) killed about 30% of the country's population.[^cite_note-114]

#### Sunnism and Shiism in pre-Safavid Iran

Main article: [Islam in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Iran)



[Imam Reza shrine](14030191.md), the tomb of the eighth Imam of the twelver Shiites

Prior to the rise of the Safavid Empire, Sunni Islam was the dominant religion, accounting for around 90% of the population at the time. According to [Mortaza Motahhari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mortaza_Motahhari) the majority of Iranian scholars and masses remained Sunni until the time of the Safavids.[^cite_note-115] The domination of Sunnis did not mean Shia were rootless in Iran. The writers of [The Four Books](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Four_Books) of Shia were Iranian, as well as many other great Shia scholars.

The domination of the Sunni creed during the first nine Islamic centuries characterized the religious history of Iran during this period. There were however some exceptions to this general domination which emerged in the form of the [Zaydīs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaidiyyah) of [Tabaristan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabaristan) (see [Alid dynasties of northern Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alid_dynasties_of_northern_Iran)), the [Buyids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buyids), the [Kakuyids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kakuyids), the rule of [Sultan Muhammad Khudabandah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%96ljeit%C3%BC) (r. Shawwal 703-Shawwal 716/1304–1316) and the [Sarbedaran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarbedaran).[^cite_note-Ja'fariyan_1998-116]

Apart from this domination there existed, firstly, throughout these nine centuries, Shia inclinations among many Sunnis of this land and, secondly, original [Imami Shiism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelvers) as well as [Zaydī Shiism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaidiyyah) had prevalence in some parts of Iran. During this period, Shia in Iran were nourished from [Kufah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kufa), [Baghdad](4492.md) and later from [Najaf](200512.md) and [Hillah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillah).[^cite_note-Ja'fariyan_1998-116] Shiism was the dominant sect in [Tabaristan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabaristan), [Qom](638742.md), [Kashan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashan), [Avaj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avaj) and [Sabzevar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabzevar). In many other areas merged population of Shia and Sunni lived together.

During the 10th and 11th centuries, [Fatimids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatimids) sent [Ismailis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ismailis) [Da'i (missioners)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dawah) to Iran as well as other Muslim lands. When Ismailis divided into two sects, [Nizaris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nizari) established their base in Iran. [Hassan-i Sabbah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hassan-i_Sabbah) conquered fortresses and captured [Alamut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alamut) in 1090 AD. Nizaris used this fortress until a Mongol raid in 1256.

After the Mongol raid and fall of the Abbasids, Sunni hierarchies faltered. Not only did they lose the caliphate but also the status of official [madhhab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madhhab). Their loss was the gain of Shia, whose centre wasn't in Iran at that time. Several local Shia dynasties like [Sarbadars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarbadars) were established during this time.

The main change occurred in the beginning of the 16th century, when [Ismail I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ismail_I) founded the [Safavid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_dynasty) and initiated a religious policy to [recognize Shi'a Islam as the official religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_conversion_of_Iran_from_Sunnism_to_Shiism) of the [Safavid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_Empire), and the fact that modern Iran remains an officially Shi'ite state is a direct result of Ismail's actions.

### Timurid Empire (1370–1507)



Forensic reconstruction of [Turco-Mongol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turco-Mongol) conqueror [Timur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timur)



Detailed map of the Timurid Empire with its [tributary states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tributary_state) and sphere of influence in [Western](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Asia)-[Central Asia](6742.md) (1402–1403)

See also: [Timurid conquests and invasions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timurid_conquests_and_invasions)

Iran remained divided until the arrival of [Timur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timur), a [Turco-Mongol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turco-Mongol_tradition)[^cite_note-118] belonging to the [Timurid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timurid_dynasty). Like its predecessors, the [Timurid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timurid_Empire) was also part of the Persianate world. After establishing a power base in Transoxiana, Timur invaded Iran in 1381 and eventually conquered most of it. Timur's campaigns were known for their brutality; many people were slaughtered and several cities were destroyed.[^cite_note-Chapin_1989-119]

His regime was characterized by tyranny and bloodshed, but also by its inclusion of Iranians in administrative roles and its promotion of architecture and poetry. His successors, the Timurids, maintained a hold on most of Iran until 1452, when they lost the bulk of it to [Black Sheep Turkmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Sheep_Turkmen). The Black Sheep Turkmen were conquered by the [White Sheep Turkmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Sheep_Turkmen) under [Uzun Hasan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uzun_Hasan) in 1468; Uzun Hasan and his successors were the masters of Iran until the rise of the Safavids.[^cite_note-Chapin_1989-119]

[Sufi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufi) poet [Hafez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafez)'s popularity became firmly established in the Timurid era that saw the compilation and widespread copying of his *[divan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divan)*. Sufis were often persecuted by orthodox Muslims who considered their teachings [blasphemous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blasphemous). Sufism developed a symbolic language rich with metaphors to obscure poetic references to provocative philosophical teachings. Hafez concealed his own Sufi faith, even as he employed the secret language of Sufism (developed over hundreds of years) in his own work, and he is sometimes credited with having "brought it to perfection".[^cite_note-120] His work was imitated by [Jami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jami), whose own popularity grew to spread across the full breadth of the Persianate world.[^cite_note-121]&#x20;



Contemporary depiction of the [Qara Qoyunlu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qara_Qoyunlu) [Pir Budaq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pir_Budaq), son of [Jahan Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jahan_Shah), c. 1455–1460

The [Kara Koyunlu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qara_Qoyunlu) were a [Turkmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkoman_\(ethnonym\))[^cite_note-122][^cite_note-126] tribal federation that ruled over northwestern Iran and surrounding areas from 1374 to 1468.
