Although the Kara Koyunlu expanded their conquest to Baghdad, internal fighting, defeats by the Timurids, rebellions by the [Armenians](387816.md) in response to their persecution,[^cite_note-127] and failed struggles with the [Ag Qoyunlu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ag_Qoyunlu) led to their eventual demise.[^cite_note-128] Aq Qoyunlu were Turkmen[^cite_note-129][^cite_note-130] under the leadership of the [Bayandur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayandur) tribe,[^cite_note-131] tribal federation of Sunni Muslims who ruled over most of Iran and large parts of surrounding areas from 1378 to 1501 CE. Aq Qoyunlu emerged when [Timur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timur) granted them all of [Diyar Bakr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diyar_Bakr) in present-day Turkey. Afterward, they struggled with their rival Oghuz Turks, the [Qara Qoyunlu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qara_Qoyunlu). While the Aq Qoyunlu were successful in defeating Kara Koyunlu, their struggle with the emerging [Safavid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_dynasty) led to their downfall.[^cite_note-132]

## Early modern period

Iran underwent a revival under the [Safavid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavids) (1501–1736), the most prominent figure of which was [Shah Abbas I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbas_I_of_Safavid). Some historians credit the Safavid dynasty for founding the modern nation-state of Iran. Iran's contemporary Shia character and significant segments of Iran's current borders take their origin from this era (*e.g. [Treaty of Zuhab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Zuhab)*).

### Safavid Empire (1501–1736)

Main article: [Safavid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_Empire)

See also: [Portuguese–Safavid wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese%E2%80%93Safavid_wars), [Safavid conversion of Iran to Shia Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_conversion_of_Iran_to_Shia_Islam), and [Ottoman–Persian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman%E2%80%93Persian_Wars)



The [Safavid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_Empire) (1501–1736) at its greatest extent

The Safavid dynasty was one of the most significant ruling dynasties of Iran and "is often considered the beginning of modern Persian history".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIranica_Online_-_Safavid_Dynasty-133] They ruled one of the greatest Iranian empires after the [Muslim conquest of Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_Persia)[^cite_note-134] and established the [Twelver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelver) school of [Shi'a Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imamate_\(Twelver_doctrine\))[^cite_note-EncIslamica_1993_VII54-14] as the official religion of their empire, marking one of the most important turning points in [Muslim history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_history). The Safavids ruled from 1501 to 1722 (experiencing a brief restoration from 1729 to 1736) and at their height, they controlled all of modern Iran, [Azerbaijan](746.md) and [Armenia](10918072.md), most of [Georgia](48768.md), the [North Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Caucasus), [Iraq](7515928.md), [Kuwait](7515890.md) and [Afghanistan](737.md), as well as parts of [Turkey](11125639.md), [Syria](7515849.md), [Pakistan](23235.md), [Turkmenistan](198149.md) and [Uzbekistan](31853.md). Safavid Iran was one of the Islamic "[gunpowder empires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gunpowder_Empires)", along with its neighbours, its archrival and principal enemy the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md), and to the east, the [Mughal Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire).



[Portuguese empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Empire) in the Persian Gulf - 1501–1750.

The Safavid ruling dynasty was founded by Ismāil, who styled himself [Shāh Ismāil I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ismail_I).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIranica_Online_-_Esmāʿīl_I_Ṣafawī-135] Practically worshipped by his [Qizilbāsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qizilbash) followers, Ismāil invaded [Shirvan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shirvan) to avenge the death of his father, [Shaykh Haydar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaykh_Haydar), who had been killed during his siege of [Derbent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Derbent), in Dagestan. Afterwards he went on a campaign of conquest, and following the capture of [Tabriz](439791.md) in July 1501, he enthroned himself as the Shāh of Iran,[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137][^cite_note-138] minted coins in this name, and proclaimed Shi'ism the official religion of his domain.[^cite_note-EncIslamica_1993_VII54-14]

Although initially the masters of Azerbaijan and southern Dagestan only, the Safavids had, in fact, won the struggle for power in Iran which had been going on for nearly a century between various dynasties and political forces following the fragmentation of the [Kara Koyunlu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kara_Koyunlu) and the [Aq Qoyunlu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aq_Qoyunlu). A year after his victory in Tabriz, Ismāil proclaimed most of Iran as his domain, and[^cite_note-EncIslamica_1993_VII54-14] quickly conquered and unified Iran under his rule. Soon afterwards, the new Safavid Empire rapidly conquered regions, nations, and peoples in all directions, including Armenia, Azerbaijan, parts of Georgia, [Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia) (Iraq), Kuwait, Syria, [Dagestan](751099.md), large parts of what is now Afghanistan, parts of Turkmenistan, and large chunks of Anatolia, laying the foundation of its multi-ethnic character which would heavily influence the empire itself (most notably the Caucasus and [its peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peoples_of_the_Caucasus)).

[Tahmasp I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahmasp_I), the son and successor of [Ismail I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ismail_I), carried out multiple invasions in the Caucasus which had been incorporated in the [Safavid empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_empire) since Shah Ismail I and for many centuries afterwards, and started with the trend of deporting and moving hundreds of thousands of [Circassians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circassians), [Georgians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgians), and [Armenians](387816.md) to Iran's heartlands. Initially only solely put in the royal harems, royal guards, and minor other sections of the Empire, Tahmasp believed he could eventually reduce the power of the [Qizilbash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qizilbash), by creating and fully integrating a new layer in Iranian society. As *[Encyclopædia Iranica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Encyclop%C3%A6dia_Iranica)* states, for Tahmasp, the problem circled around the military tribal elite of the empire, the Qizilbash, who believed that physical proximity to and control of a member of the immediate Safavid family guaranteed spiritual advantages, political fortune, and material advancement.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIranica_Online_-_Ṭahmāsp_I-139] With this new Caucasian layer in Iranian society, the undisputed might of the Qizilbash (who functioned much like the *[ghazis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazi_\(warrior\))* of the neighbouring [Ottoman Empire](22278.md)) would be questioned and fully diminished as society would become fully [meritocratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meritocracy).



Portrait of [Shah Abbas I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah_Abbas_I). [Chehel Sotoun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chehel_Sotoun), painted c. 1647.

[^cite_note-140]

Shah [Abbas I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbas_I_of_Persia) and his successors would significantly expand this policy and plan initiated by Tahmasp, deporting during his reign alone around some 200,000 [Georgians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgians), 300,000 [Armenians](387816.md) and 100,000–150,000 [Circassians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circassians) to Iran, completing the foundation of a new layer in Iranian society. With this, and the complete systematic disorganisation of the Qizilbash by his personal orders, he eventually fully succeeded in replacing the power of the Qizilbash, with that of the [Caucasian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peoples_of_the_Caucasus) ghulams. These new Caucasian elements (the so-called *[ghilman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghilman)* / غِلْمَان / *"servants"*), almost always after conversion to [Shi'ism](26961.md) depending on given function would be, unlike the Qizilbash, fully loyal only to the Shah. The other masses of Caucasians were deployed in all other possible functions and positions available in the empire, as well as in the [harem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harem), regular military, craftsmen, farmers, etc. This system of mass usage of Caucasian subjects remained to exist until the fall of the [Qajar dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qajar_dynasty).

The greatest of the Safavid monarchs, [Shah Abbas I the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah_Abbas_I_the_Great) (1587–1629) came to power in 1587 aged 16. Abbas I first fought the Uzbeks, recapturing [Herat](14128.md) and Mashhad in 1598, which had been lost by his predecessor [Mohammad Khodabanda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Khodabanda) by the [Ottoman–Safavid War (1578–1590)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman%E2%80%93Safavid_War_\(1578%E2%80%931590\)). Then he turned against the Ottomans, the archrivals of the Safavids, recapturing Baghdad, eastern Iraq, the Caucasian provinces, and beyond [by 1618](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman%E2%80%93Safavid_War_\(1603%E2%80%931618\)). Between 1616 and 1618, following the disobedience of his most loyal Georgian subjects [Teimuraz I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teimuraz_I) and [Luarsab II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luarsab_II), Abbas carried out a punitive campaign in his territories of Georgia, devastating [Kakheti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kakheti) and [Tbilisi](42298.md) and carrying away 130,000[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMunshi1978\[httpsarchiveorgdetailsmonshi-shah-abbas-englishMonshi_Shah-Abbas_Englishpage1116mode1up_Volume_1_p._1116\]-141] – 200,000[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMikaberidze2015\[httpsbooksgooglecabooksidJNNQCgAAQBAJpgPA291_291\],_\[httpsbooksgooglecabooksidJNNQCgAAQBAJpgPA536_536\]-142][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIranica_Online_-_GEORGIA_vii.\[httpswwwiranicaonlineorgarticlesgeorgia-vii-_Georgians_in_the_Safavid_Administration\]-143] Georgian captives towards mainland Iran. His new army, which had dramatically been improved with the advent of [Robert Shirley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Shirley) and his brothers following the [first diplomatic mission to Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_embassy_to_Europe_\(1599%E2%80%931602\)), pitted the first crushing victory over the Safavids' archrivals, the Ottomans in the above-mentioned 1603–1618 war and would surpass the Ottomans in military strength. He also used his new force to dislodge the Portuguese from [Bahrain](18933277.md) (1602) and [Hormuz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hormuz_Island) (1622) with aid of the English navy, in the Persian Gulf.

He expanded commercial links with the [Dutch East India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_East_India_Company) and established firm links with the European royal houses, which had been initiated by Ismail I earlier on by the [Habsburg–Persian alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habsburg%E2%80%93Persian_alliance). Thus Abbas I was able to break the dependence on the Qizilbash for military might and therefore was able to centralize control. The Safavid dynasty had already established itself during Shah Ismail I, but under Abbas I it really became a major power in the world along with its archrival the Ottoman Empire, against whom it became able to compete with on equal foot. It also started the promotion of tourism in Iran. Under their rule Persian Architecture flourished again and saw many new monuments in various Iranian cities, of which [Isfahan](223149.md) is the most notable example.

Except for Shah [Abbas the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbas_the_Great), Shah [Ismail I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ismail_I), Shah [Tahmasp I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahmasp_I), and Shah [Abbas II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbas_II_of_Persia), many of the Safavid rulers were ineffectual, often being more interested in their women, alcohol and other leisure activities. The end of Abbas II's reign in 1666, marked the beginning of the end of the Safavid dynasty. Despite falling revenues and military threats, many of the later shahs had lavish lifestyles. Shah [Soltan Hoseyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soltan_Hoseyn) (1694–1722) in particular was known for his love of wine and disinterest in governance.[^cite_note-144]

The declining country was repeatedly raided on its frontiers. Finally, [Ghilzai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghilzai) [Pashtun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtun_people) chieftain named [Mir Wais Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirwais_Hotak) began a rebellion in [Kandahar](17260.md) and defeated the Safavid army under the Iranian Georgian governor over the region, [Gurgin Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurgin_Khan). In 1722, [Peter the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_the_Great) of neighbouring [Imperial Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Russia) launched the [Russo-Persian War (1722–1723)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Persian_War_\(1722%E2%80%931723\)), capturing many of Iran's Caucasian territories, including [Derbent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Derbent), [Shaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaki,_Azerbaijan), [Baku](4566.md), but also [Gilan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilan), [Mazandaran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazandaran) and [Astrabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astrabad). In the midst of chaos, in the same year of 1722, an Afghan army led by Mir Wais' son [Mahmud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmud_Hotak) marched across eastern Iran, [besieged and took Isfahan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Isfahan). Mahmud proclaimed himself 'Shah' of Persia. Meanwhile, Persia's imperial rivals, the Ottomans and the Russians, took advantage of the chaos in the country to seize more territory for themselves.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAxworthy2006\[httpsbooksgooglecabooksidDCSPDwAAQBAJpgPA17_17–56\]-145] By these events, the Safavid dynasty had effectively ended. In 1724, conform the [Treaty of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Constantinople_\(1724\)), the Ottomans and the Russians agreed to divide large portions of Iran, which they had conquered between themselves.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMikaberidze20111024-146]

### Nader Shah and his successors

Main articles: [Afsharid Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afsharid_Iran), [Afsharid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afsharid_dynasty), [Campaigns of Nader Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campaigns_of_Nader_Shah), [Zand Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zand_Iran), and [Zand dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zand_dynasty)



Portrait of [Nader Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nader_Shah), c. 1740



The [Afsharid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afsharid_Iran) at its greatest extent in 1741–1745 under [Nader Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nader_Shah)

Iran's territorial integrity was restored by a native Iranian [Turkic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_people) [Afshar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afsharid) warlord from Khorasan, [Nader Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nader_Shah). He defeated and banished the Afghans, [defeated the Ottomans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman%E2%80%93Persian_War_\(1730%E2%80%9335\)), [reinstalled the Safavids on the throne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restoration_of_Tahmasp_II_to_the_Safavid_throne), and negotiated Russian withdrawal from Iran's Caucasian territories, with the [Treaty of Resht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Resht) and [Treaty of Ganja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Ganja). By 1736, Nader had become so powerful he was able to depose the Safavids and have himself crowned shah. Nader was one of the last great conquerors of Asia and briefly presided over what was probably the most powerful military force in the world.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAxworthy2006xv-147] To financially support his wars against Iran's arch-rival, the Ottoman Empire, he fixed his sights on the weak but rich Mughal Empire to the east. In 1739, accompanied by his loyal Caucasian subjects including [Heraclius II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heraclius_II_of_Georgia),[^cite_note-148][^cite_note-Suny1994_55-149] he [invaded Mughal India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nader_Shah's_invasion_of_India), defeated a numerically superior Mughal army in [less than three hours](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Karnal), and completely sacked and looted Delhi, bringing back immense wealth to Iran. On his way back, he also conquered all the Uzbek khanates – except for [Kokand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kokand) – and made the Uzbeks his vassals. He also firmly re-established Iranian rule over the entire Caucasus, Bahrain, as well as large parts of Anatolia and Mesopotamia. Undefeated for years, his defeat in [Dagestan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nader_Shah's_Dagestan_campaign), following guerrilla rebellions by the [Lezgins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lezgins) and the assassination attempt on him near [Mazandaran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazandaran_province) is often considered the turning point in Nader's impressive career. To his frustration, the Dagestanis resorted to guerrilla warfare, and Nader with his conventional army could make little headway against them.[^cite_note-150] At the [Battle of Andalal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_through_Andalal_\(1741\)) and the Battle of Avaria, Nader's army was crushingly defeated and he lost half of his entire force, forcing him to flee for the mountains.[^cite_note-151] Though Nader managed to take most of Dagestan during his campaign, the effective guerrilla warfare as deployed by the Lezgins, but also the [Avars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avar_people_\(Caucasus\)) and [Laks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gazikumukh_Khanate), made the Iranian re-conquest of the particular [North Caucasian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Caucasus) region this time a short lived one; several years later, Nader [was forced to withdraw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nader_Shah's_Dagestan_campaign). Around the same time, an assassination attempt was made on him near, which accelerated his descent into paranoia and megalomania. He blinded his sons, whom he suspected of the assassination attempts, and showed increasing cruelty against his subjects and officers. In his later years, this eventually provoked multiple revolts and, ultimately, his assassination in 1747.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAxworthy2010\[httpsarchiveorgdetailshistoryofiranemp0000axwo_n7v2page152mode1up_152–67\]-152]

Nader Shah's death was followed by a period of anarchy as [rival army commanders fought for power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Division_of_the_Afsharid_Empire). Nader's own family, the Afsharids, were soon reduced to holding on to a small domain in Khorasan. Many of the Caucasian territories broke away in various [Caucasian khanates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khanates_of_the_Caucasus). Ottomans regained lost territories in Anatolia and Mesopotamia. [Oman](22316.md) and the Uzbek khanates of [Bukhara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bukhara) and [Khiva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khiva) regained independence. [Ahmad Shah Durrani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Shah_Durrani), one of Nader's officers, founded an independent state which eventually became modern Afghanistan. Heraclius II and [Teimuraz II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teimuraz_II), who in 1744 had been made the kings of [Kakheti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kakheti) and [Kartli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kartli) respectively by Nader for their loyal service,[^cite_note-Suny1994_55-149] capitalized on the eruption of instability and declared *de facto* independence. Heraclius II assumed control over Kartli after Teimuraz II's death, thus unifying the two as the [Kingdom of Kartli-Kakheti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kartli-Kakheti), becoming the first Georgian ruler in three centuries to preside over a politically unified eastern Georgia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIranica_Online_-_Erekle_II-153] Due to the frantic turn of events in mainland Iran he would be able to remain *de facto* autonomous through the [Zand era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zand_Iran).[^cite_note-CHI_vol7_3289-154]

From his capital [Shiraz](39622.md), [Karim Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karim_Khan_Zand) of the [Zand dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zand_dynasty) ruled "an island of relative calm and peace in an otherwise bloody and destructive period,"[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAxworthy2010\[httpsarchiveorgdetailshistoryofiranemp0000axwo_n7v2page168mode1up_168\]-155] however the extent of Zand power was confined to contemporary Iran and parts of the Caucasus. Karim Khan's death in 1779 led to yet another civil war in which the [Qajar dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qajar_dynasty) eventually triumphed and became kings of Iran. During the civil war, Iran permanently lost [Basra](57580.md) in 1779 to the Ottomans, which had been captured during the [Ottoman–Persian War (1775–1776)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman%E2%80%93Persian_War_\(1775%E2%80%931776\)),[^cite_note-156] and [Bahrain](18933277.md) to the [House of Khalifa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Khalifa) after the [Bani Utbah invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bani_Utbah_invasion_of_Bahrain) in 1783.

## Late modern period

### Qajar dynasty (1796–1925)

Main articles: [Qajar Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qajar_Iran), [Qajar dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qajar_dynasty), [Russo-Persian War (1804–1813)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Persian_War_\(1804%E2%80%931813\)), and [Russo-Persian War (1826–1828)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Persian_War_\(1826%E2%80%931828\))

See also: [Battle of Krtsanisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Krtsanisi), [Treaty of Gulistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Gulistan), [Treaty of Turkmenchay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Turkmenchay), and [Iranian Constitutional Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Constitutional_Revolution)

  Qajar era currency bill with depiction of [Naser al-Din Shah Qajar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naser_al-Din_Shah_Qajar)

- A map of Iran under the Qajar dynasty in the 19th century

  A map of Iran under the [Qajar dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qajar_dynasty) in the 19th century

- A map showing the 19th-century northwestern borders of Iran, comprising modern-day eastern Georgia, Dagestan, Armenia, and the Republic of Azerbaijan, before being ceded to the neighboring Russian Empire by the Russo-Iranian wars

  A map showing the 19th-century northwestern borders of Iran, comprising modern-day eastern [Georgia](48768.md), [Dagestan](751099.md), [Armenia](10918072.md), and the [Republic of Azerbaijan](746.md), before being ceded to the neighboring [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire) by the [Russo-Iranian wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Persian_Wars)

[Agha Mohammad Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agha_Mohammad_Khan) emerged victorious out of the civil war that commenced with the death of the last Zand king. His reign is noted for the reemergence of a centrally led and united Iran. After the death of Nader Shah and the last of the Zands, most of Iran's Caucasian territories had broken away into various [Caucasian khanates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khanates_of_the_Caucasus). Agha Mohammad Khan, like the Safavid kings and Nader Shah before him, viewed the region as no different from the territories in mainland Iran. Therefore, his first objective after having secured mainland Iran, was to reincorpate the Caucasus region into Iran.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMikaberidze2011409-157] Georgia was seen as one of the most integral territories.[^cite_note-CHI_vol7_3289-154] For Agha Mohammad Khan, the resubjugation and reintegration of Georgia into the Iranian Empire was part of the same process that had brought Shiraz, [Isfahan](223149.md), and Tabriz under his rule.[^cite_note-CHI_vol7_3289-154] As the *[Cambridge History of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambridge_History_of_Iran)* states, its permanent secession was inconceivable and had to be resisted in the same way as one would resist an attempt at the separation of [Fars](448049.md) or [Gilan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilan_Province).[^cite_note-CHI_vol7_3289-154] It was therefore natural for Agha Mohammad Khan to perform whatever necessary means in the Caucasus in order to subdue and reincorporate the recently lost regions following Nader Shah's death and the demise of the Zands, including putting down what in Iranian eyes was seen as treason on the part Erekle II.[^cite_note-CHI_vol7_3289-154]

Agha Mohammad Khan subsequently demanded that Erekle renounce [its 1783 treaty with Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Georgievsk), and to submit again to Iranian [suzerainty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suzerainty),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMikaberidze2011409-157] in return for peace and the security of his kingdom. The Ottomans, Iran's neighboring rival, recognized the latter's rights over [Kartli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kartli) and [Kakheti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kakheti) for the first time in four centuries.[^cite_note-Rayfield2013_255-158] Heraclius appealed then to his theoretical protector, Empress [Catherine II of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catherine_II_of_Russia), pleading for at least 3,000 Russian troops,[^cite_note-Rayfield2013_255-158] but he was ignored, leaving Georgia to fend off the Persian threat alone.[^cite_note-Lang1962_38-159] Nevertheless, Heraclius II still rejected the Khan's [ultimatum](198432.md).[^cite_note-Suny1994_59-160] As a response, Agha Mohammad Khan invaded the Caucasus region after crossing the [Aras river](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aras_river), and, while on his way to Georgia, he re-subjugated Iran's territories of the [Erivan Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erivan_Khanate), [Shirvan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shirvan), [Nakhchivan Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nakhchivan_Khanate), [Ganja khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganja_khanate), [Derbent Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Derbent_Khanate), [Baku khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baku_khanate), [Talysh Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talysh_Khanate), [Shaki Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaki_Khanate), [Karabakh Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karabakh_Khanate), which comprise modern-day [Armenia](10918072.md), [Azerbaijan](746.md), [Dagestan](751099.md), and [Igdir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I%C4%9Fd%C4%B1r_Province). Having reached Georgia with his large army, he prevailed in the [Battle of Krtsanisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Krtsanisi), which resulted in the capture and sack of [Tbilisi](42298.md), as well as the effective resubjugation of Georgia.[^cite_note-Axworthy2010_192-161][^cite_note-163] Upon his return from his successful campaign in Tbilisi and in effective control over Georgia, together with some 15,000 Georgian captives that were moved back to mainland Iran,[^cite_note-Lang1962_38-159] Agha Mohammad was formally crowned [Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah) in 1796 in the [Mughan plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughan_plain), just as his predecessor Nader Shah was about sixty years earlier. Agha Mohammad Shah was later assassinated in 1797 while preparing a second expedition against Georgia in [Shusha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shusha)[^cite_note-CHI_vol7_3289-154] (now part of the [Republic of Azerbaijan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Azerbaijan)) and its King Heraclius II.

The reassertion of Iranian hegemony over Georgia did not last long; in 1799 the Russians marched into Tbilisi.[^cite_note-164] The Russians were already actively occupied with an expansionist policy towards its neighboring empires to its south, namely the Ottoman Empire and the successive Iranian kingdoms, since the late 17th/early 18th century. The next two years following Russia's entrance into Tbilisi were a time of confusion, and the weakened and devastated Georgian kingdom, with its capital half in ruins, was easily [absorbed by Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_within_the_Russian_Empire) in 1801.[^cite_note-Lang1962_38-159][^cite_note-Suny1994_59-160] As Iran could not permit or allow the cession of [Transcaucasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcaucasia) and Dagestan, which had been an integral part of Iran for centuries,[^cite_note-CHI_vol7_32930-165] this would lead directly to the wars of several years later, namely the [Russo-Persian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Persian_Wars) of [1804-1813](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Persian_War_\(1804%E2%80%931813\)) and [1826–1828](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Persian_War_\(1826%E2%80%931828\)). The outcome of these two wars (in the [Treaty of Gulistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Gulistan) and the [Treaty of Turkmenchay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Turkmenchay), respectively) proved for the irrevocable forced cession and loss of what is now eastern Georgia, Dagestan, Armenia, and Azerbaijan to Imperial Russia.[^cite_note-CHI_vol7_32930-165][^cite_note-Axworthy2010_192-161]

The area to the north of the river [Aras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aras_\(river\)), among which the territory of the contemporary republic of Azerbaijan, eastern Georgia, Dagestan, and Armenia were Iranian territory, was occupied by Russia in the course of the 19th century.[^cite_note-166]
