### Migration of Caucasian Muslims

See also: [Ayrums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayrums), [Qarapapaqs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qarapapaqs), and [Ethnic Cleansing of Circassians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_Cleansing_of_Circassians)



Persian Cossack Brigade in [Tabriz](439791.md) in 1909

Following the official loss of vast territories in the Caucasus, major demographic shifts were bound to take place. Following the 1804–1814 war, but also per the 1826–1828 war which ceded the last territories, large migrations of so-called Caucasian Muhajirs set off for mainland Iran. Some of these groups included the [Ayrums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayrums), [Qarapapaqs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qarapapaqs), [Circassians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circassians), Shia [Lezgins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lezgins), and other [Transcaucasian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcaucasus) Muslims.[^cite_note-Yemelianova_2013-167]

After the [Battle of Ganja of 1804](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Ganja_\(1804\)), many thousands of Ayrums and Qarapapaqs were settled in Tabriz. During the remaining part of the 1804–1813 war, as well as through the [1826–1828 war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Persian_War_\(1826%E2%80%931828\)), a large number of the Ayrums and Qarapapaqs that were still remaining in newly conquered Russian territories were settled in and migrated to [Solduz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solduz) (in modern-day Iran's [West Azerbaijan province](47567329.md)).[^cite_note-Mansoori-168] As the *[Cambridge History of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambridge_History_of_Iran)* states; "The steady encroachment of Russian troops along the frontier in the Caucasus, General [Yermolov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleksey_Yermolov_\(general\))'s brutal punitive expeditions and misgovernment, drove large numbers of Muslims, and even some [Georgian Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Georgia_\(country\)), into exile in Iran."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Cambridge_History_of_Iran_Vol._7_Ch._9336-169]

From 1864 until the early 20th century, [another mass expulsion took place](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_cleansing_of_Circassians) of Caucasian Muslims as a result of the Russian victory in the [Caucasian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasian_War). Others simply voluntarily refused to live under Christian Russian rule, and thus departed for Turkey or Iran. These migrations once again, towards Iran, included masses of Caucasian [Azerbaijanis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijanis), other Transcaucasian Muslims, as well as many North Caucasian Muslims, such as Circassians, Shia Lezgins and [Laks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lak_people_\(Dagestan\)).[^cite_note-Yemelianova_2013-167][^cite_note-170] Many of these migrants would prove to play a pivotal role in further Iranian history, as they formed most of the ranks of the [Persian Cossack Brigade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_Cossack_Brigade), which was established in the late 19th century.[^cite_note-Hashim_June2012-171] The initial ranks of the brigade would be entirely composed of [Circassians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circassians) and other Caucasian Muhajirs.[^cite_note-Hashim_June2012-171] This brigade would prove decisive in the following decades in Qajar history.

Furthermore, the 1828 [Treaty of Turkmenchay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Turkmenchay) included the official rights for the Russian Empire to encourage settling of [Armenians](387816.md) from Iran in the newly conquered Russian territories.[^cite_note-175] At the close of the fourteenth century, after [Timur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timur)'s campaigns, the [Timurid Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timurid_Renaissance) flourished and Islam had become the dominant faith. Armenians had by then become a minority in Eastern Armenia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBournoutian198011,_13–4-174] After centuries of constant warfare on the [Armenian plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_plateau), many Armenians chose to emigrate and settle elsewhere. Following [Shah Abbas I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah_Abbas_I)'s massive relocation of Armenians and Muslims in 1604–05,[^cite_note-177] their numbers dwindled even further.

At the time of the Russian invasion of Iran, some 80% of the population of [Iranian Armenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Armenia_\(1502%E2%80%931828\)) were Muslims ([Persians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_people), [Turkics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_peoples), and [Kurds](17068.md)) whereas Christian [Armenians](387816.md) constituted a minority of about 20%.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBournoutian198012–3-178] As a result of the [Treaty of Gulistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Gulistan) (1813) and the Treaty of Turkmenchay (1828), Iran was forced to cede Iranian Armenia (which also constituted the present-day [Armenia](10918072.md)), to the Russians.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBournoutian19801–2-179][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMikaberidze2015\[httpsbooksgooglecabooksidJNNQCgAAQBAJpgPA141_141\]-180] After the Russian administration took hold of Iranian Armenia, the ethnic make-up shifted, and thus for the first time in more than four centuries, ethnic Armenians started to form a majority once again in one part of historic Armenia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBournoutian198014-181] The new Russian administration encouraged the settling of ethnic Armenians from Iran proper and [Ottoman Turkey](22278.md). As a result, by 1832, the number of ethnic Armenians had matched that of the Muslims.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBournoutian198012–3-178] It would be only after the [Crimean War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_War) and the [Russo-Turkish War of 1877–1878](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Turkish_War_\(1877%E2%80%931878\)), which brought another influx of Turkish Armenians, that ethnic Armenians once again established a solid majority in [Eastern Armenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Armenia).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBournoutian198013-182] Nevertheless, the city of Erivan retained a Muslim majority up to the twentieth century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBournoutian198013-182] According to the traveller [H. F. B. Lynch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H._F._B._Lynch), the city of Erivan was about 50% Armenian and 50% Muslim (Tatars[^cite_note-185] i.e. Azeris and Persians) in the early 1890s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIranica_Online_-_Erevan-186]

[Fath Ali Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fath_Ali_Shah)'s reign saw increased diplomatic contacts with the West and the beginning of intense European diplomatic rivalries over Iran. His grandson [Mohammad Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Shah_Qajar), who succeeded him in 1834, fell under the Russian influence and made two unsuccessful attempts to capture [Herat](14128.md). When Mohammad Shah died in 1848 the succession passed to his son [Naser al-Din Shah Qajar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naser_al-Din_Shah_Qajar), who proved to be the ablest and most successful of the Qajar sovereigns. He founded the first modern hospital in Iran.[^cite_note-187]

### Constitutional Revolution and deposition

Main article: [1921 Persian coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1921_Persian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat)



The [V-Dem Democracy Indices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V-Dem_Democracy_Indices) for electoral democracy (solid) and liberal democracy (dotted)

[^cite_note-VDemDataV16-188]

&#x20;show Iranian electoral democracy starting with the Constitutional Revolution.

The [Great Persian Famine of 1870–1871](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_famine_of_1870%E2%80%931872) is believed to have caused the death of two million people.[^cite_note-189]

A new era in the history of Iran dawned with the [Persian Constitutional Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_Constitutional_Revolution) against the shah in the late 19th and early 20th centuries. The shah managed to remain in power, granting a limited constitution in 1906 (making the country a [constitutional monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_monarchy)). The first [Majlis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majlis) (parliament) was convened on 7 October 1906. The discovery of [petroleum](23195.md) in 1908 by the British in [Khuzestan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kh%C5%ABzest%C4%81n_Province) spawned intense renewed interest in Persia by the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) (see [William Knox D'Arcy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Knox_D'Arcy) and [Anglo-Iranian Oil Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Iranian_Oil_Company), now [BP](18998720.md)). Britain's influence was solidified by the establishment of the Indo-European Telegraph Department in the 1860s and the [Imperial Bank of Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Bank_of_Persia) in 1889.[^cite_note-190] By the end of the 19th century, European interference became so pronounced that Iran's central government required Anglo-Russian approval for ministerial appointments.[^cite_note-191] Control of Persia remained contested between the United Kingdom and Russia, in what became known as [The Great Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Great_Game), and codified in the [Anglo-Russian Convention of 1907](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Russian_Convention_of_1907), which divided Iran into spheres of influence, regardless of her national sovereignty.

During World War I, the country was occupied by British, Ottoman and Russian forces but was essentially neutral (see [Persian Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_campaign_\(World_War_I\))). In 1919, after the [Russian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Revolution) and their withdrawal, Britain attempted to establish a [protectorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectorate) in Iran, which was unsuccessful. The [Constitutionalist movement of Gilan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutionalist_movement_of_Gilan) and the central power vacuum caused by the instability of the Qajar government resulted in the rise of Reza Khan, later [Reza Shah Pahlavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reza_Shah_Pahlavi), who established the [Pahlavi dynasty](338883.md) in 1925. In 1921, Reza Khan, an officer of the [Persian Cossack Brigade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_Cossack_Brigade), (along with [Seyyed Zia'eddin Tabatabai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seyyed_Zia'eddin_Tabatabai)) led a military coup against governing officials (leaving the Qajar monarchy nominally head of state).[^cite_note-193] In 1925, after being prime minister for two years, Reza Khan did depose the Qajar dynasty and became the first shah of the Pahlavi dynasty.

### Pahlavi era (1925–1979)

Main article: [Pahlavi Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pahlavi_Iran)

#### Reza Shah (1925–1941)

Main article: [Persian Cossack Brigade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_Cossack_Brigade)

Reza Shah ruled for almost 16 years until 16 September 1941, when he was forced to [abdicate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdication) by the [Anglo-Soviet invasion of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Soviet_invasion_of_Iran). He established an [authoritarian government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarianism) that valued [nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_nationalism), [militarism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Militarism), [secularism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism_in_Iran) and [anti-communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-communism) combined with strict [censorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship) and [state propaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_propaganda).[^cite_note-194] Reza Shah introduced many socio-economic reforms, reorganizing the army, government administration, and finances.[^cite_note-195] To his supporters, his reign brought "law and order, discipline, central authority, and modern amenities – schools, trains, buses, radios, cinemas, and telephones".[^cite_note-Abrahamian2008_91-196] However, his attempts of modernisation have been criticised for being "too fast"[^cite_note-197] and "superficial",[^cite_note-198] and his reign a time of "oppression, corruption, taxation, lack of authenticity" with "security typical of [police states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_state)."[^cite_note-Abrahamian2008_91-196]

Many of the new laws and regulations created resentment among devout Muslims and the clergy. For example, mosques were required to use chairs; most men were required to wear western clothing, including a hat with a brim; women were encouraged to discard the [hijab](68301.md)—hijab was eventually [banned in 1936](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashf-e_hijab); men and women were allowed to congregate freely, violating Islamic [mixing of the sexes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_segregation_and_Islam). Tensions boiled over in 1935, when bazaaris and villagers rose up [in rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goharshad_Mosque_rebellion) at the [Imam Reza shrine](14030191.md) in [Mashhad](406512.md) to protest against plans for the hijab ban, chanting slogans such as 'The Shah is a new [Yezid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yazid_I).' Dozens were killed and hundreds were injured when troops finally quelled the unrest.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakhash198422-199]

#### World War II

See also: [Anglo-Soviet invasion of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Soviet_invasion_of_Iran), [Polish civilian camps in World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_civilian_camps_in_World_War_II), and [Persian Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_Corridor)



[Mohammad Reza Pahlavi](20065598.md) with FDR at the [Tehran Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehran_Conference), 1943.



[Polish refugee camp on the outskirts of Tehran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evacuation_of_Polish_civilians_from_the_USSR_in_World_War_II#Iran_and_the_Middle_East), c. 1943.

While [German armies were highly successful](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Barbarossa) against the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union_in_World_War_II), the Iranian government expected Germany to win the war and establish a powerful force on its borders. It rejected British and Soviet demands to expel German residents from Iran. In response, the two [Allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II) [invaded in August 1941](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Soviet_invasion_of_Iran) and easily overwhelmed the weak Iranian army in *[Operation Countenance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Countenance)*. Iran became the major conduit of Allied [Lend-Lease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lend-Lease) aid to the Soviet Union. The purpose was to secure Iranian [oil fields](2910801.md) and ensure Allied [supply lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supply_line) (see *[Persian Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_Corridor)*). Iran remained officially neutral. Its monarch [Rezā Shāh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rez%C4%81_Sh%C4%81h) was deposed during the subsequent occupation and replaced with his young son [Mohammad Reza Pahlavi](20065598.md).[^cite_note-200]

At the [Tehran Conference of 1943](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehran_Conference_of_1943), the Allies issued the [Tehran Declaration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehran_Declaration) which guaranteed the post-war independence and boundaries of Iran. However, when the war actually ended, Soviet troops stationed in northwestern Iran not only refused to withdraw but backed revolts that established short-lived, pro-Soviet separatist national states in the northern regions of Azerbaijan and [Iranian Kurdistan](61733686.md), the [Azerbaijan People's Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijan_People's_Government) and the [Republic of Kurdistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Kurdistan) respectively, in late 1945. Soviet troops did not withdraw from Iran proper until May 1946 after receiving a promise of oil concessions. The Soviet republics in the north were soon [overthrown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_crisis_of_1946) and the oil concessions were revoked.[^cite_note-201][^cite_note-202]

#### Mohammad-Reza Shah (1941–1979)



Tehran men celebrating the [1953 Iranian coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1953_Iranian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat)

Initially there were hopes that post-occupation Iran could become a [constitutional monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_monarchy). The new, young Shah [Mohammad Reza Shah Pahlavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Reza_Shah_Pahlavi) initially took a very hands-off role in government, and allowed [parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament) to hold a lot of power. Some elections were held in the first shaky years, although they remained mired in corruption. Parliament became chronically unstable, and from the 1947 to 1951 period Iran saw the rise and fall of six different prime ministers. Pahlavi increased his political power by convening the [Iran Constituent Assembly, 1949](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_Constituent_Assembly,_1949), which finally formed the [Senate of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_of_Iran)—a legislative [upper house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_house) allowed for in the 1906 constitution but never brought into being. The new senators were largely supportive of Pahlavi, as he had intended.

In 1951 Prime Minister [Mohammed Mosaddeq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Mosaddeq) received the vote required from the parliament to [nationalize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalize) the British-owned oil industry, in a situation known as the [Abadan Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abadan_Crisis). Despite British pressure, including an economic blockade, the nationalization continued. Mosaddeq was briefly removed from power in 1952 but was quickly re-appointed by the Shah, due to a popular uprising in support of the premier, and he, in turn, forced the Shah into a brief exile in August 1953 after a failed military coup by [Imperial Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Guard_\(Iran\)) Colonel [Nematollah Nassiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nematollah_Nassiri).

##### 1953: U.S. aided coup removes Mosaddeq

Main article: [1953 Iranian coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1953_Iranian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat)

Shortly thereafter on 19 August a successful [coup](25914154.md) was headed by retired army general [Fazlollah Zahedi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fazlollah_Zahedi), aided by the United States ([CIA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA))[^cite_note-203] with the active support of the British ([MI6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MI6)) (known as [Operation Ajax and Operation Boot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1953_Iranian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) to the respective agencies).[^cite_note-204] The coup—with a [black propaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_propaganda) campaign designed to turn the population against Mosaddeq[^cite_note-205] — forced Mosaddeq from office. Mosaddeq was arrested and tried for treason. Found guilty, his sentence was reduced to house arrest on his family estate while his foreign minister, [Hossein Fatemi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hossein_Fatemi), was executed. [Zahedi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fazlollah_Zahedi) succeeded him as prime minister, and suppressed opposition to the Shah, specifically the [National Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Front_\(Iran\)) and Communist [Tudeh Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tudeh_Party). Several hundred people were killed after the coup d'état.

1971 film about Iran under the Shah

Iran was ruled as an autocracy under the Shah with American support from that time until the revolution. The Iranian government entered into agreement with an international consortium of foreign companies which ran the Iranian oil facilities for the next 25 years, splitting profits fifty-fifty with Iran but not allowing Iran to audit their accounts or have members on their board of directors. In 1957 martial law was ended after 16 years and Iran became closer to the West, joining the [Baghdad Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baghdad_Pact) and receiving military and economic aid from the US. In 1961, Iran initiated a series of economic, social, agrarian and administrative reforms to modernize the country that became known as the Shah's [White Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Revolution).

The core of this program was land reform. Modernization and economic growth proceeded at an unprecedented rate, fueled by Iran's vast petroleum reserves, the third-largest in the world. However, the reforms, including the White Revolution, did not greatly improve economic conditions and the liberal pro-Western policies alienated certain [Islamic](6037917.md) religious and political groups. In early June 1963 [several days of massive rioting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Movement_of_15_Khordad) occurred in support of [Ayatollah](63899.md) [Ruhollah Khomeini](26232.md) following the cleric's arrest for a speech attacking the Shah.

Two years later, premier [Hassan Ali Mansur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hassan_Ali_Mansur) was assassinated and the internal security service, [SAVAK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SAVAK), became more violently active. In the 1970s, leftist [guerilla groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guerrilla_groups_of_Iran) such as [Mujaheddin-e-Khalq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mujaheddin-e-Khalq) (MEK), emerged and contributed to overthrowing the Shah during the 1979 Iranian Revolution.



Shah [Mohammad Reza Pahlavi](20065598.md) with US President [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon) in Tehran, Iran, 30 May 1972

Nearly a hundred Iranian political prisoners were killed by the SAVAK during the decade before the revolution and many more were arrested and tortured.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1999\[httpsarchiveorgdetailstorturedconfessi0000abrapage135mode1up_135–6\],_\[httpsarchiveorgdetailstorturedconfessi0000abrapage167mode1up_167\],_169-206] The Islamic clergy, headed by the Ayatollah [Ruhollah Khomeini](26232.md) (who had been exiled in 1964), were becoming increasingly vociferous.

Iran greatly increased its defense budget and by the early 1970s was the region's strongest military power. Bilateral relations with Iraq were not good, mainly due to a dispute over the [Shatt al-Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shatt_al-Arab) waterway. In November 1971, Iranian forces seized control of [three islands at the mouth of the Persian Gulf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seizure_of_Abu_Musa_and_the_Greater_and_Lesser_Tunbs); in response, Iraq expelled thousands of Iranian nationals. Following a number of clashes in April 1969, Iran abrogated [the 1937 accord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Saadabad) and demanded a renegotiation.

In mid-1973, the Shah returned the oil industry to national control. Following the [Arab-Israeli War of October 1973](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yom_Kippur_War), Iran did not join the Arab oil embargo against the West and [Israel](9282173.md). Instead, it used the situation to raise oil prices, using the money gained for modernisation and to increase defense spending.

A border dispute between Iraq and Iran was resolved with the signing of the [Algiers Accord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algiers_Agreement_\(1975\)) on 6 March 1975.

## Contemporary period

### Revolution and the Islamic Republic (1979 to present)

Main articles: [Iranian Revolution](347268.md) and [History of the Islamic Republic of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Islamic_Republic_of_Iran)



Ayatollah [Khomeini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khomeini) returns to Iran after 14 years exile in France on 1 February 1979.

The Iranian Revolution, also known as the Islamic Revolution,[^cite_note-207] was the [revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolution) that transformed Iran from an absolute [monarchy](338883.md) under [Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah) [Mohammad Reza Pahlavi](20065598.md) to an [Islamic republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_republic) under [Ayatollah](63899.md) [Ruhollah Khomeini](26232.md), one of the leaders of the revolution and founder of the Islamic Republic.[^cite_note-EB_Afary_2026-18] Its time span can be said to have begun in January 1978 with the first major demonstrations,[^cite_note-208] and concluded with the approval of the new [theocratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theocratic) Constitution—whereby Ayatollah Khomeini became [Supreme Leader](36814344.md) of the country—in December 1979.[^cite_note-Brit_Khomeini-209]

In between, [Mohammad Reza Pahlavi](20065598.md) left the country for exile in January 1979 after strikes and demonstrations paralyzed the country, and on 1 February 1979, Ayatollah Khomeini returned to Tehran.[^cite_note-Brit_Khomeini-209] The final collapse of the Pahlavi dynasty occurred shortly after on 11 February when Iran's military declared itself "neutral" after guerrillas and rebel troops overwhelmed troops loyal to the Shah in armed street fighting. Iran officially became an Islamic Republic on 1 April 1979, after Iranians overwhelmingly approved a [national referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_Iranian_Islamic_Republic_referendum) to make it so a day before.[^cite_note-Brit_Iran-17]

#### Ideology of the 1979 Iranian Revolution

Further information: [Ideology of the Iranian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ideology_of_the_Iranian_Revolution)

The ideology of the revolutionary government was [populist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populism), nationalist and most of all [Shi'a Islamic](26961.md). Its unique constitution is based on the concept of *[velayat-e faqih](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Government)* the idea advanced by Khomeini that Muslims – in fact everyone – requires "guardianship", in the form of rule or supervision by the leading [Islamic jurist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faqih) or jurists.[^cite_note-210] Khomeini served as this ruling jurist, or [supreme leader](36814344.md), until his death in 1989.

Iran's rapidly modernising, capitalist economy was replaced by populist and Islamic economic and cultural policies. Much industry was [nationalized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalized), laws and schools Islamicized, and Western influences banned.

The Islamic revolution also created great impact around the world. In the non-Muslim world it has changed the image of Islam, generating much interest in the politics and spirituality of Islam,[^cite_note-211] along with "fear and distrust towards Islam" and particularly the Islamic Republic and its founder.[^cite_note-nasr-212]

### Khomeini (1979–1989)

Khomeini served as leader of the revolution or as [Supreme Leader of Iran](36814344.md) from 1979 to his death on 3 June 1989. This era was dominated by the consolidation of the revolution into a [theocratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theocracy) republic under Khomeini, and by the costly and bloody [war with Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War).

Revolutionary factions disagreed on the shape of the new Iran. Those who thought the Shah would be replaced by a democratic government soon found Khomeini disagreed. In early March 1979, he announced, "do not use this term, 'democratic.' That is the Western style."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakhash198473-213] In succession the [National Democratic Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democratic_Front_\(Iran\)) was banned in August 1979, the [provisional government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Interim_Government_of_Iran) was disempowered in November, the [Muslim People's Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_People's_Republican_Party) banned in January 1980, the [People's Mujahedin of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Mujahedin_of_Iran) (MEK) and its supporters came under attack between 1979 and 1981, a purge of universities was begun in March 1980, and leftist President [Abolhassan Banisadr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolhassan_Banisadr) was impeached in June 1981.[^cite_note-214]

The consolidation lasted until 1982–3,[^cite_note-215][^cite_note-216] as Iran coped with the damage to its economy, military, and apparatus of government. Protests and uprisings by secularists, leftists, and more traditional Muslims—formerly allied revolutionaries but now rivals—were effectively suppressed. Many political opponents were executed by the new regimes. Following the events of the revolution, Marxist guerrillas and federalist parties revolted in regions comprising [Khuzistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_Khuzestan_uprising), [Kurdistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_Kurdish_rebellion_in_Iran) and [Gonbad-e Qabus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gonbad-e_Qabus_\(city\)), resulting in severe fighting between rebels and revolutionary forces. These revolts began in April 1979 and lasted between several months to over a year, depending on the region. The [Kurdish uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_Kurdish_rebellion_in_Iran), led by the KDPI, was the most violent, lasting until 1983 and resulting in 10,000 casualties.

In the summer of 1979 a new constitution giving Khomeini a powerful post as guardian jurist Supreme Leader[^cite_note-217] and a clerical [Council of Guardians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Guardians) power over legislation and elections, was drawn up by an [Assembly of Experts for Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assembly_of_Experts_for_Constitution). The new constitution was approved by referendum in December 1979.

#### Iran hostage crisis (1979–1981)

Main article: [Iran hostage crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_hostage_crisis)

An early event in the history of the Islamic republic that had a long-term impact was the [Iran hostage crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_hostage_crisis). Following the admitting of the former Shah of Iran into the United States for cancer treatment, on 4 November 1979, Iranian students [seized US embassy personnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_hostage_crisis), labeling the embassy a "den of spies."[^cite_note-218] Fifty-two hostages were held for 444 days until January 1981.[^cite_note-219] An American [military attempt to rescue the hostages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Eagle_Claw) failed.[^cite_note-220]

The takeover was enormously popular in Iran, where thousands gathered in support of the hostage takers, and it is thought to have strengthened the prestige of the [Ayatollah Khomeini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayatollah_Khomeini) and consolidated the hold of anti-Americanism. It was at this time that Khomeini began referring to America as the "[Great Satan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Satan)." In America, where it was considered a violation of the long-standing principle of international law that [diplomats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diplomatic_immunity) may be expelled but not held captive, it created a powerful anti-Iranian backlash. Relations between the two countries have remained deeply antagonistic and American [international sanctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_sanctions) have hurt Iran's economy.[^cite_note-221]

#### Iran–Iraq War (1980–1988)

Main article: [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War)

See also: [1988 executions of Iranian political prisoners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_executions_of_Iranian_political_prisoners) and [Political repression in the Islamic Republic of Iran](80259436.md)



An Iranian soldier with gas mask during the Iran–Iraq War

During this political and social crisis, Iraqi leader [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein) attempted to take advantage of the disorder of the Revolution, the weakness of the Iranian military and the revolution's antagonism with Western governments. The once-strong Iranian military had been disbanded during the revolution, and with the Shah ousted, Hussein had ambitions to position himself as the new strong man of the Middle East. He sought to expand Iraq's access to the Persian Gulf by acquiring territories that Iraq had claimed earlier from Iran during the Shah's rule.

Of chief importance to Iraq was [Khuzestan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khuzestan_Province) which not only boasted a substantial Arab population, but rich oil fields as well. On the unilateral behalf of the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md), the islands of [Abu Musa](931858.md) and the [Greater and Lesser Tunbs](931816.md) became objectives as well. With these ambitions in mind, Hussein planned a full-scale assault on Iran, boasting that his forces could reach the capital within three days. On 22 September 1980, the Iraqi army invaded Iran at Khuzestan, precipitating the [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War). The attack took revolutionary Iran completely by surprise.

Although Saddam Hussein's forces made several early advances, Iranian forces had pushed the Iraqi army back into Iraq by 1982. Khomeini sought to [export his Islamic revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Export_of_revolution) westward into Iraq, especially on the majority Shi'a Arabs living in the country. The war then continued for six more years until 1988, when Khomeini, in his words, "drank the cup of poison" and accepted a truce mediated by the United Nations.

Tens of thousands of Iranian civilians and military personnel were killed when Iraq used [chemical weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemical_weapons) in its warfare. [Iraq was financially backed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_aid_to_combatants_in_the_Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War) by [Egypt](8087628.md), the Arab countries of the [Persian Gulf](24761.md), the Soviet Union and the [Warsaw Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Pact) states, the United States (beginning in 1983), France, the United Kingdom, Germany, [Brazil](3383.md), and the [People's Republic of China](5405.md) (which also sold weapons to Iran).

There were more than 182,000 Kurdish victims[^cite_note-222] of Iraq's chemical weapons during the eight-year war. The total Iranian casualties of the war were estimated to be between 500,000 and 1,000,000. Almost all relevant international agencies have confirmed that Saddam engaged in chemical warfare to blunt Iranian [human wave attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_wave_attack); these agencies unanimously confirmed that Iran never used chemical weapons during the war.[^cite_note-223]

Starting on 19 July 1988 and lasting for about five months the [government](14653.md) systematically executed thousands of political prisoners across Iran. This is commonly referred to as the [1988 executions of Iranian political prisoners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_executions_of_Iranian_political_prisoners) or the 1988 Iranian Massacre. The main target was the membership of the [People's Mojahedin Organization of Iran](285632.md) (PMOI), although a lesser number of political prisoners from other leftist groups were also included such as the [Tudeh Party of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tudeh_Party_of_Iran) (Communist Party).[^cite_note-224][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1999\[httpsarchiveorgdetailstorturedconfessi0000abrapage209mode1up_209–28\]-225] Estimates of the number executed vary from 1,400[^cite_note-226] to 30,000.[^cite_note-227][^cite_note-228]

### Khamenei (1989–2026)

On his deathbed in 1989, [Khomeini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khomeini) appointed a 25-man Constitutional Reform Council which named then president [Ali Khamenei](385653.md) as the next Supreme Leader, and made a number of changes to Iran's constitution.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian2008\[httpsarchiveorgdetailshistoryofmoderni0000abrapage182mode1up_182\]-229] A smooth transition followed Khomeini's death on 3 June 1989. While Khamenei lacked Khomeini's "charisma and clerical standing", he developed a network of supporters within Iran's armed forces and its economically powerful [religious foundations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonyad).[^cite_note-Abrahamian2008_LRB-230] Under his reign Iran's regime is said – by at least one observer – to resemble more "a clerical oligarchy ... than an autocracy."[^cite_note-Abrahamian2008_LRB-230]

#### Rafsanjani: pragmatic conservativism (1989–1997)

Ali-[Akbar Hashemi Rafsanjani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akbar_Hashemi_Rafsanjani) succeeded Khamenei as president on 3 August 1989, as a pragmatic conservative who served two four-year terms and focused his efforts on rebuilding the country's economy and infrastructure damaged by war, though hampered by low oil prices. Rafsanjani sought to restore confidence in the government among the general population by privatizing the companies that had been nationalized in the first few years of the Islamic Republic, as well as by bringing in qualified technocrats to manage the economy. The state of their economy also influenced the government to move towards ending their diplomatic isolation. This was achieved through the reestablishment of normalized relations with neighbors such as [Saudi Arabia](349303.md) and an attempt to improve its reputation in the region with assertions that its revolution was not exportable to other states.[^cite_note-Parsi2007_145-231] During the [Persian Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War) in 1991 the country remained [neutral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutral_country), restricting its action to the condemnation of the U.S. and allowing fleeing Iraqi aircraft and refugees into the country.[^cite_note-Parsi2007_145-231]

Iran in the 1990s had a greater secular behavior and admiration for Western popular culture than in the previous decades. This admiration had become a way in which the urban population expressed their resentment at the invasive Islamic policies of the government.[^cite_note-CleveBunt2016_5078-232] The pressures from the population placed on the new Supreme Leader Ayatollah [Ali Khamenei](385653.md) led to an uneasy alliance between him and President [Akbar Hashemi Rafsanjani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akbar_Hashemi_Rafsanjani). Through this alliance they attempted to hinder the [ulama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulama)'s ability to gain further control of the state. In 1989, they created a sequence of constitutional amendments that removed the office of prime minister and increased the scope of presidential power. However, these new amendments did not curtail the powers of the [Supreme Leader of Iran](36814344.md) in any way; this position still contained the ultimate authority over the armed forces, the making of war and peace, the final say in foreign policy, and the right to intervene in the legislative process whenever he deemed it necessary.[^cite_note-CleveBunt2016_5078-232]

#### Khatami: reformers and conservatives struggle (1997–2005)

Main article: [Presidency of Muhammad Khatami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Muhammad_Khatami)



[Mohammad Khatami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Khatami)

President Rafsanjani's economic policies led to stronger relations with the outside world. But his government's relaxation of the enforcement of certain regulations on social behavior were met with some responses of widespread disenchantment among the general population with the ulama as rulers of the country.[^cite_note-CleveBunt2016_5078-232] This led to the defeat of the government's candidate for president in 1997, who had the backing of the supreme Islamic jurist. He was beaten by an independent candidate from the [Reformists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Reformists), [Mohammad Khatami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Khatami). He received 69% of the vote and enjoyed particular support from two groups of the population that had felt ostracized by the practices of the state: women and youth. The younger generations in the country had been too young to experience the shah's regime or the revolution that ended it, and now they resented the restrictions placed on their daily lives under the Islamic Republic. Mohammad Khatami's presidency was soon marked by tensions between the [reform-minded government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Khatami's_reforms) and an increasingly conservative and vocal clergy. This rift reached a climax in July 1999 when massive anti-government protests erupted in the streets of [Tehran](57654.md). The disturbances lasted over a week before police and pro-government vigilantes dispersed the crowds.

During his first term, President Khatami oversaw Iran's second five-year development plan and introduced a new plan for 2000–2004 focused on [economic reconstruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_reconstruction) alongside social and political reforms. The plan aimed for privatization, job creation, and reduced subsidies but fell short on employment targets.[^cite_note-233] Despite this, Iran saw improved economic indicators: real GDP growth rose to nearly 6 percent, unemployment and inflation declined, external debt dropped significantly, and the government authorized private banks for the first time since 1979.[^cite_note-234] Poverty levels also decreased modestly.[^cite_note-235]

In the [Majlis elections of 2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Iranian_legislative_election), for the first time liberals and Khatami's supporters gained parliamentary control from conservatives.[^cite_note-Iran_BBC_20200106-236] That same year, following the adoption of a new press law, authorities banned the publication of 16 reformist newspapers.[^cite_note-Iran_BBC_20200106-236]

Khatami was re-elected in June 2001 but his efforts were repeatedly blocked by the conservatives in the parliament. Conservative elements within Iran's government moved to undermine the reformist movement, banning liberal newspapers and disqualifying candidates for parliamentary elections. This clampdown on dissent, combined with the failure of Khatami to reform the government, led to growing political apathy among Iran's youth.

Following the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md) in 2001, Iran initially was sympathetic with the United States.[^cite_note-237] However, relations deteriorated sharply after President [George W. Bush](3414021.md) labeled Iran part of the "[Axis of Evil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_of_Evil)" in 2002, accusing the country of pursuing weapons of mass destruction that posed a threat to the U.S.[^cite_note-238][^cite_note-fax-239]

Despite firm U.S. opposition, in 2002 Russian teams commenced work on Iran's inaugural [nuclear reactor at Bushehr.](18522794.md)[^cite_note-Iran_BBC_20200106-236]

In June 2003, anti-government protests by several thousand students took place in Tehran.[^cite_note-240][^cite_note-241] [Shirin Ebadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shirin_Ebadi), a lawyer and human rights advocate, became the first Iranian to win the [Nobel Peace Prize](26230922.md) in 2003. She had been the country's first female judge until being forced to step down after the 1979 revolution.[^cite_note-Iran_BBC_20200106-236] The response to the award in Iran was mixed—enthusiastic supporters greeted her at the airport upon her return, the conservative media underplayed it, and Khatami criticized it as political.[^cite_note-242][^cite_note-243]

A violent [earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_Bam_earthquake) struck the [Kerman province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kerman_province) of southeastern Iran in December 2003. The earthquake was particularly destructive in [Bam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bam,_Iran), with the death toll amounting to at least 34,000 people and injuring up to 200,000.[^cite_note-244]

After the hardline [Council of Guardians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Guardians) disqualified thousands of reformist candidates, conservatives regained control of parliament in the [elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Iranian_legislative_election) of 2004.[^cite_note-Iran_BBC_20200106-236]

#### Ahmadinejad: conservatism (2005–2013)



[Mahmoud Ahmadinejad](7907317.md)

In the [2005 Iranian presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_Iranian_presidential_election), [Mahmoud Ahmadinejad](7907317.md), mayor of Tehran, became the sixth president of Iran, after winning 62 percent of the vote in the [run-off poll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Runoff_election), against former president Ali-[Akbar Hashemi Rafsanjani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akbar_Hashemi_Rafsanjani).[^cite_note-245] During the authorization ceremony he kissed Khamenei's hand in demonstration of his loyalty to him.[^cite_note-246]

During this time, the [American invasion of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_invasion_of_Iraq), the overthrow of [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein)'s regime and empowerment of its [Shi'a](26961.md) majority, all strengthened Iran's position in the region particularly in the mainly Shi'a south of Iraq, where a top Shia leader in the week of the 3rd. of September 2006 renewed demands for an autonomous Shi'a region.[^cite_note-247] At least one commentator (former U.S. Defense Secretary [William S. Cohen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_S._Cohen)) has stated that as of 2009, Iran's growing power had eclipsed [anti-Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Zionism) as the major foreign policy issue in the Middle East.[^cite_note-248]

During 2005 and 2006, there were claims that [the United States](310477.md#Claims_of_threats_of_a_military_attack_on_Iran_by_the_US) and [Israel](3022965.md#Iran_responding_to_Israeli_threats) were planning to attack Iran, with the most cited reason being [Iran's civilian nuclear energy program](721807.md) which the United States and some other states feared could lead to [a nuclear weapons program](1757165.md#Nuclear_weapons). China and Russia opposed military action of any sort and also opposed [economic sanctions](411315.md). Khamenei issued a [fatwa](11259.md) forbidding the production, stockpiling and use of [nuclear weapons](21785.md). The fatwa was cited in an official statement by the Iranian government at an August 2005 meeting of the [International Atomic Energy Agency](14984.md) (IAEA) in [Vienna](55866.md).[^cite_note-249][^cite_note-250] However, the IAEA reported in 2008 that Iran's suspected nuclear weapons research remained “a matter of serious concern,” prompting European Union countries to agree on new sanctions.[^cite_note-251] Additional U.N. sanctions followed in 2010.[^cite_note-Iran_BBC_20200106-236] In 2011, Iran announced that the [Bushehr Nuclear Power Plant](18522794.md) had been connected to the national electricity grid for the first time.[^cite_note-Iran_BBC_20200106-236] Eventually, the sanctions severely impacted Iran's economy, contributing to a dramatic depreciation of the [rial](708656.md), which reportedly fell to a record low of 35,000 to the US dollar—an 80% drop since late 2011.[^cite_note-252]

In 2007, a diplomatic standoff erupted between Iran and the UK after [Iranian forces detained](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Iranian_arrest_of_Royal_Navy_personnel) 15 British sailors and marines near the Shatt al-Arab waterway, which forms part of the Iran-Iraq border.[^cite_note-Iran_BBC_20200106-236]

In 2009, [Ahmadinejad's reelection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_presidential_election,_2009) was hotly disputed and marred by large [protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Iranian_presidential_election_protests) that formed the "greatest domestic challenge" to the leadership of the Islamic Republic "in 30 years". The resulting social unrest is widely known as the [Iranian Green Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Green_Movement). Reformist opponent [Mir-Hossein Mousavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mir-Hossein_Mousavi) and his supporters alleged voting irregularities and by 1 July 2009, 1000 people had been arrested and 20 killed in street demonstrations.[^cite_note-253] Supreme Leader [Ali Khamenei](385653.md) and other Islamic officials blamed foreign powers for fomenting the protest.[^cite_note-254]

In 2010, [Stuxnet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stuxnet) was reportedly found in the [Natanz Nuclear Facility](43648558.md).[^cite_note-Iran_BBC_20200106-236] Stuxnet is a [malicious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malware) [computer worm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer_worm)[^cite_note-255] thought to have been in development since at least 2005. Stuxnet targets [supervisory control and data acquisition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supervisory_control_and_data_acquisition) (SCADA) systems and is believed to be responsible for causing substantial damage to the [Iran nuclear program](721807.md).[^cite_note-256] Although neither the [United States](3434750.md) nor [Israel](9282173.md) has openly admitted responsibility, multiple independent news organizations claim Stuxnet to be a [cyberweapon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyberweapon) built jointly by the two countries in a collaborative effort known as [Operation Olympic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Olympic_Games).[^cite_note-257] The program, started during the [Bush administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_George_W._Bush), was rapidly expanded within the first months of [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama)'s presidency.[^cite_note-258]

On 14 February 2011, [widespread protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011%E2%80%932012_Iranian_protests) erupted in Tehran as thousands gathered in response to opposition calls, expressing solidarity with pro-democracy movements in the region and reviving dissent over the contested 2009 presidential election. Security forces quickly suppressed the demonstrations, resulting in two deaths and numerous injuries. Further protests followed, including on 20 February and 1 March, when the opposition reported around 200 arrests. Authorities subsequently managed to prevent large-scale demonstrations.[^cite_note-MEp_BBC_20120713-259]

Reports of growing tensions between Ahmadinejad and Khamenei emerged during this period. In the 2012 parliamentary elections, Ahmadinejad's allies lost ground to factions loyal to Khamenei, while the opposition Green Movement remained banned. Its leaders, [Mehdi Karroubi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehdi_Karroubi) and Mir-Hossein Mousavi, were placed under house arrest in early 2011 and have remained out of public view, with some government supporters demanding their execution.[^cite_note-MEp_BBC_20120713-259]s

#### Rouhani: pragmatism (2013–2021)

See also: [Iran nuclear deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_nuclear_deal) and [United States withdrawal from the Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_withdrawal_from_the_Joint_Comprehensive_Plan_of_Action)



Hassan Rouhani 2017

On 15 June 2013, [Hassan Rouhani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hassan_Rouhani) won the presidential election in Iran, with a total number of 36,704,156 ballots cast; Rouhani won 18,613,329 votes. In his press conference one day after election day, Rouhani reiterated his promise to recalibrate Iran's relations with the world.[^cite_note-260]

On 14 July 2015, after years of negotiations, Iran and the [P5+1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P5+1) group of world powers (China, France, Russia, the United Kingdom, the United States, plus Germany)[^cite_note-261] together with the European Union finalized the [Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Comprehensive_Plan_of_Action) (JCPOA), commonly known as the Iran nuclear deal.[^cite_note-262] The agreement aimed to limit Iran's nuclear program in exchange for relief from international sanctions.[^cite_note-263] It followed the 2013 [Joint Plan of Action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Plan_of_Action), an interim deal that opened formal negotiations.[^cite_note-264] By April 2015, negotiators had agreed on a framework that set the stage for the final accord in Vienna.[^cite_note-265]

Under the JCPOA, Iran agreed to significant restrictions on its nuclear activities, including limits on uranium enrichment levels, the number and type of operating centrifuges, and the size of its enriched uranium stockpile. Key facilities at Fordow, Natanz, and Arak were to be repurposed for civilian research and medical uses. Iran also accepted more intrusive inspections by the International Atomic Energy Agency to verify compliance. In return, it received relief from nuclear-related sanctions imposed by the United Nations, the European Union, and the United States, although many other U.S. sanctions remained in place, especially those targeting Iran's missile program and regional activities.[^cite_note-266]

Beginning on 28 December 2017, protests known as the [Dey protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017%E2%80%932018_Iranian_protests) spread across Iran, starting over economic grievances in [Mashhad](406512.md) but quickly expanding to political opposition to Supreme Leader [Ali Khamenei](385653.md) and the theocratic system.[^cite_note-267] Marking the most serious unrest since 2009, the largely leaderless protests[^cite_note-268] featured anti-regime chants and attacks on government sites,[^cite_note-269] with at least twenty-one protesters and two security personnel killed, and around 3,700 arrested by early January 2018.[^cite_note-270] In response, thousands of government supporters held pro-government rallies in multiple cities.[^cite_note-271]

In May 2018, Donald Trump decided to [pull out of the JCPOA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_withdrawal_from_the_Joint_Comprehensive_Plan_of_Action), announcing he would reimpose economic sanctions on Iran effective from 4 November that year.[^cite_note-272] This marked the beginning of the Trump administration's [maximum pressure campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximum_pressure_campaign), an effort to force Iran to renegotiate the nuclear agreement by imposing intensified sanctions.[^cite_note-273]

On 22 September 2018, the [Ahvaz military parade was attacked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahvaz_military_parade_attack) by gunmen in the southwestern Iranian city of [Ahvaz](599079.md).[^cite_note-274] The shooters killed 25 people, including soldiers of the [Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps](1285472.md) (IRGC) and civilian bystanders.[^cite_note-275] The [Islamic State](9087364.md) claimed responsibility for the attack.[^cite_note-276] Iran blamed "militants in Syria"[^cite_note-277] and claimed the "U.S. and the Gulf states enabled the attack" and vowed revenge.[^cite_note-278] The U.S., Saudi Arabia and the United Arab Emirates rejected the accusation.[^cite_note-279]

From mid-March to April 2019 widespread [flash flooding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Iran_floods) affected large parts of [Iran](14653.md), most severely in [Golestan](448067.md), [Fars](448049.md), [Khuzestan](448079.md), [Lorestan](433072.md), and other provinces. Iran was hit by three major waves of rain and flooding over the course of two weeks[^cite_note-280] which led to flooding in at least 26 of Iran's 31 provinces.[^cite_note-NYT_20190406-281] At least 70 people died nationwide.[^cite_note-NYT_20190406-281]

The [2019–2020 Iranian protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019%E2%80%932020_Iranian_protests) began in response to a 50–200% fuel price increase[^cite_note-282] and quickly spread to 21 cities,[^cite_note-283] becoming the most violent unrest since the 1979 revolution.[^cite_note-284][^cite_note-NYT_FasGla_20191201-285] Security forces reportedly shot protesters from rooftops, helicopters, and at close range, killing around 1,500 people according to U.S. sources,[^cite_note-286] while [Amnesty International](18947898.md) described efforts to cover up the scale of the violence.[^cite_note-287] Protesters attacked 731 banks, 50 military bases, and nine religious centers,[^cite_note-288][^cite_note-NYT_FasGla_20191201-285] prompting the government to impose a near-total internet blackout for six days.[^cite_note-289] The uprising was crushed within three days,[^cite_note-291] though sporadic protests continued.

On 3 January 2020, the United States military executed a drone strike at [Baghdad Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baghdad_Airport), [killing Qasem Soleimani](62726668.md), the leader of the [Quds Force](4028355.md), an elite branch of the Iranian [Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps](1285472.md) (IRGC).[^cite_note-292] The assassination sharply increased tensions between the two countries. Iran vowed retaliation, and on 8 January launched [missile attacks on U.S. forces based in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Martyr_Soleimani), marking the first direct military exchange between Iran and the U.S. since 1988. The same day, the IRGC mistakenly shot down [Ukraine International Airlines Flight 752](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukraine_International_Airlines_Flight_752). Following these events, no further military escalation occurred.[^cite_note-293]

The [2020 parliamentary elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Iranian_legislative_election) in Iran were marked by historically low voter turnout, officially reported at 42.6%—the lowest since the 1979 revolution. The elections took place in the wake of widespread public disillusionment following the violent crackdown on protests in late 2019, which severely damaged the credibility of President Hassan Rouhani and the reformist camp. As a result, conservative candidates won a dominant majority in the parliament, securing 221 out of 290 seats, while reformists managed to win only a small fraction. The outcome was widely seen as a significant blow to Rouhani ahead of the end of his term in 2021.[^cite_note-294]

The [COVID-19 pandemic in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Iran) led to 7,627,863[^cite_note-Template\:COVID-19_data-295] confirmed cases of [COVID-19](63030231.md) and 146,837[^cite_note-Template\:COVID-19_data-295] deaths. The first cases were reported in Qom on 19 February 2020.[^cite_note-296] The government responded by cancelling public events, closing institutions and shrines,[^cite_note-297][^cite_note-BBC_20200324-298] and requesting a \$5 billion emergency loan from the [IMF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IMF).[^cite_note-TNY_Filkins_20200518-290] Initial resistance to quarantines and travel restrictions contributed to the virus's spread before a ban on intercity travel was implemented.[^cite_note-BBC_20200324-298][^cite_note-299] After restrictions eased in April, cases surged again, peaking in June and July.[^cite_note-300] Despite these rising case numbers, the government had no option but to keep the economy open, as it was already under strain from U.S. sanctions and had suffered a further 15% GDP decline due to the pandemic by June 2020.[^cite_note-301] Estimates of deaths have varied widely, with some leaked data suggesting a much higher toll than official figures,[^cite_note-302] and the government faced allegations of mismanagement and censorship.[^cite_note-303] The virus also impacted Iran's leadership, infecting 23 MPs by early March and killing at least 17 officials by late March.[^cite_note-304]

#### Ebrahim Raisi (2021–2024)



[Ebrahim Raisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ebrahim_Raisi) in 2021

On 3 August 2021 [Ebrahim Raisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ebrahim_Raisi) was elected [8th President of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Iranian_presidential_election).[^cite_note-305]

On 16 September 2022, 22-year-old Iranian woman [Mahsa Amini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahsa_Amini) died in a hospital in [Tehran](57654.md), Iran, under suspicious circumstances.[^cite_note-306] The [Guidance Patrol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guidance_Patrol), the [religious morality police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_religious_police) of [Iran's government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran's_government), had arrested Amini for allegedly not wearing the [hijab](68301.md) in accordance with government standards. The [Law Enforcement Command of the Islamic Republic of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_Enforcement_Command_of_the_Islamic_Republic_of_Iran) stated that she had a heart attack at a police station, collapsed, and fell into a coma before being transferred to a hospital.[^cite_note-307] However, eyewitnesses reported that she was severely beaten and that she died as a result of [police brutality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_brutality),[^cite_note-308] which was denied by the Iranian authorities.[^cite_note-309] Amini's death resulted in [a series of protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahsa_Amini_protests) described as more widespread and larger than previous large protests.[^cite_note-310] [Iran Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_Human_Rights) reported that by December 2022 at least 476 people had been killed by security forces attacking protests across the country.[^cite_note-311] By spring 2023, the protests had largely subsided,[^cite_note-312] ultimately leaving the political leadership unchanged and firmly entrenched in power.[^cite_note-313]

In October 2023, an IAEA report estimated Iran had increased its uranium stockpile 22 times over the 2015 agreed JCPOA limit.[^cite_note-314]

On 1 April 2024, Israel's air [strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_bombing_of_the_Iranian_embassy_in_Damascus) on an Iranian consulate building in the Syrian capital [Damascus](8914.md) killed an important senior commander of the [Islamic Revolutionary Guards Corps](1285472.md) (IRGC), Brig Gen [Mohammad Reza Zahedi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Reza_Zahedi).[^cite_note-315] In retaliation for the Israeli strike, Iran [attacked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/April_2024_Iranian_strikes_against_Israel) Israel with over 300 drones and missiles on 13 April. However, the Iranian attack was mainly intercepted either outside Israeli airspace or over the country itself. It was the biggest missile attack in Iranian history, and its first ever direct attack on Israel.[^cite_note-316] It was followed by a retaliatory [missile strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/April_2024_Israeli_strikes_on_Iran) by Israel on Isfahan, Iran on 19 April.[^cite_note-317]

On 19 May 2024, Ebrahim Raisi died in a [helicopter crash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Varzaqan_helicopter_crash) in the country's East Azerbaijan province.[^cite_note-318] First Vice President [Mohammad Mokhber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Mokhber) was appointed acting president after the death of President Raisi.[^cite_note-319]

#### Masoud Pezeshkian (2024–present)



[Masoud Pezeshkian](2650383.md)

On 28 July 2024, [Masoud Pezeshkian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Masoud_Pezeshkian) was formally endorsed as Iran's new president by Iran's supreme leader, Ayatollah Ali Khamenei. Pezeshkian, a reformist, won in a [presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Iranian_presidential_election) runoff on 5 July.[^cite_note-320] Three days later, [Ismail Haniyeh](41207463.md), political chief of Palestinian political and military organisation [Hamas](13913.md), was [assassinated](77478235.md) in Iran's capital, Tehran, where he was to attend the inauguration ceremony of Iran's President Masoud Pezeshkian.[^cite_note-321]

On 1 October 2024, Iran launched about 180 ballistic missiles at Israel in retaliation for assassinations of Haniyeh, [Hassan Nasrallah](313436.md) and [Abbas Nilforoushan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbas_Nilforoushan). On 27 October, Israel responded to that attack by strikes on a missile defence system in the Iranian region of Isfahan.[^cite_note-322]

In December 2024, the [fall of the Assad regime](78548066.md) in Syria, a close ally of Iran, was a severe setback for the political influence of Iran in the region.[^cite_note-323]



Protest in Tehran against [Israeli strikes on Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/June_2025_Israeli_strikes_on_Iran), 20 June 2025

In early 2025, Iran was enriching substantial quantities of uranium to 60% purity, close to weapons-grade. Analysts warned that such activity exceeded any plausible civilian justification.[^cite_note-324] Beginning in April 2025, Iran and the United States entered [negotiations for a new nuclear agreement](79685207.md), but progress stalled as Iran's leaders have refused to stop enriching uranium.[^cite_note-325] Among the main points of disagreement were the conditions for lifting [sanctions against Iran](9941859.md).[^cite_note-326] In June 2025, IAEA found Iran non-compliant with its nuclear obligations for the first time in two decades.[^cite_note-327] In response, Iran announced the activation of a new enrichment facility and began installing additional advanced centrifuges.[^cite_note-328]

On 13 June 2025, Israel launched coordinated strikes across Iran, targeting nuclear facilities and eliminating top members of Iran's military leadership. This was the beginning of the [Twelve-Day War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve-Day_War)[^cite_note-329] Iran retaliated with waves of missile and drone strikes against Israeli cities and military sites.[^cite_note-330] [United States strikes on Iranian nuclear sites](80262844.md) occurred on 22 June 2025.[^cite_note-331] On 24 June, Israel and Iran agreed to a [ceasefire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve-Day_War_ceasefire) after insistence from the US.[^cite_note-332]

Beginning on 28 December 2025, [mass demonstrations erupted](81976180.md) across multiple cities in Iran amid widespread dissatisfaction with the Islamic Republic government and a deepening economic crisis. The movement quickly became the largest outbreak of unrest in Iran since the 2022–2023 protests following the death of Mahsa Amini.[^cite_note-333] The ensuing crackdown, carried out under [Ali Khamenei](385653.md)'s and senior officials' order for live fire on protesters, resulted in [massacres that left thousands of protesters dead](82036668.md).[^cite_note-334] The Iranian government faced accusations of committing [crimes against humanity](61099.md).[^cite_note-335]

On 28 February 2026, the [United States](3434750.md) and [Israel](9282173.md) launched a [major attack on Iran](82546969.md) with the stated goal of [regime change](154074.md).[^cite_note-336] The attack included [the assassination](82537558.md) of Supreme Leader [Ali Khamenei](385653.md), [whose compound](51025254.md) was destroyed, as well as [Ali Shamkhani](262775.md), former head of Iran's [Supreme National Security Council](6295948.md), and [several other Iranian officials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Iranian_officials_killed_during_the_2026_Iran_conflict). In retaliation, Iran launched dozens of its drones and ballistic missiles throughout the [Persian Gulf](24761.md) in addition to targeting Israel.[^cite_note-JINSA_2026-03-02-337]

[^cite_note-44]: &#x20;"It is the autochthones of the Iranian plateau, and not the Proto-Indo-European tribes of Europe, which are, in the main, the ancestors, in the physical sense of the word, of the present-day Iranians."<sup>[\[43\]](#cite_note-43)</sup>

[^cite_note-79]: &#x20;"Babak's Iranianizing rebellion in Azerbaijan gave occasion for sentiments at the capital to harden against men who were sympathetic to the more explicitly Iranian tradition".<sup>[\[77\]](#cite_note-78)</sup>

[^cite_note-81]: &#x20;"The activities of the Khurammiya reached their peak in the movement of Babak al-Khurrami, whose protracted rebellion based in north-western Iran seriously threatened the stability of the Abbassid caliphate... This revolt lasting for more than twenty years soon spread from Azerbaijan (North/West Iran) to western and central parts of Iran."<sup>[\[78\]](#cite_note-80)</sup>

[^cite_note-83]: &#x20;"Babak revolted in Azerbaijan (816–838), evoking Abu Muslim as a heroic symbol... and called for a return to the Iranian past."<sup>[\[79\]](#cite_note-82)</sup>

[^cite_note-fn_Brit_Seljuq-87]: &#x20;"... Because the Turkish Seljuqs had no Islamic tradition or strong literary heritage of their own, they adopted the cultural language of their Persian instructors in Islam. Literary Persian thus spread to the whole of Iran, and the Arabic language disappeared in that country except in works of religious scholarship ..."<sup>[\[82\]](#cite_note-86)</sup>

[^cite_note-102]: &#x20;"Chinggis Khan organized a unit of Chinese catapult specialists in 1214, and these men formed part of the first Mongol army to invade Transoxania in 1219. This was not too early for true firearms, and it was nearly two centuries after catapult-thrown gunpowder bombs had been added to the Chinese arsenal. Chinese siege equipment saw action in Transoxania in 1220 and in the north Caucasus in 1239–40."<sup>[\[96\]](#cite_note-101)</sup>

[^cite_note-104]: &#x20;"Though he was himself a Chinese, he learned his trade from his father, who had accompanied Genghis Khan on his invasion of Muslim Transoxania and Iran. Perhaps the use of gunpowder as a propellant, in other words the invention of true guns, appeared first in the Muslim Middle East, whereas the invention of gunpowder itself was a Chinese achievement."<sup>[\[97\]](#cite_note-103)</sup>

[^cite_note-106]: &#x20;"During the 1250s, the Mongols invaded Iran with 'whole regiments' of Chinese engineers operating trebuchets (catapults) throwing gunpowder bombs. Their progress was rapid and devastating until, after the sack of Baghdad in 1258, they entered Syria. There they met an Islamic army similarly equipped and experienced their first defeat. In 1291, the same sort of weapon was used during the siege of Acre, when the European Crusaders were expelled form Palestine."<sup>[\[98\]](#cite_note-Pacey1991_46-105)</sup>

[^cite_note-108]: &#x20;"Indeed, it is possible that gunpowder devices, including Chinese mortar (huochong), had reached Central Asia through the Mongols as early as the thirteenth century. Yet the potential remained unexploited; even Sultan Husayn's use of cannon may have had Ottoman inspiration."<sup>[\[99\]](#cite_note-107)</sup>

[^cite_note-109]: &#x20;"The presence of these individuals in China in the 1270s, and the deployment of Chinese engineers in Iran, mean that there were several routes by which information about gunpowder weapons could pass from the Islamic world to China, or vice versa. Thus when two authors from the eastern Mediterranean region wrote books about gunpowder weapons around the year 1280, it is not surprising that they described bombs, rockets and fire-lances very similar to some types of Chinese weaponry.<sup>[\[98\]](#cite_note-Pacey1991_46-105)</sup>

[^cite_note-118]: &#x20;"He was born some 100 km (62 miles) south of Samarkand into a clan of the Barlas, a Turkicized tribe of Mongol descent."<sup>[\[107\]](#cite_note-117)</sup>

[^cite_note-126]: &#x20;"Subsequently, it came under the control of Turkmen dynasties like the Āq Qoyunlū and Qara Qoyunlū and then of local khanates like those of Qara Bāḡ and Naḵǰavān which formed a buffer region between the Ottomans and Safavids."<sup>[\[112\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEIranica_Online_-_Araxes_River-123)</sup>"In a state of demographic stagnation or downturn, the region was an easy prey for nomadic Turkmen. The Turkmen, however, never managed to build strong states, owing to a lack of sedentary populations (Martinez-Gros 2009: 643). When Tamerlane died in 1405, the Jalāyerid sultan Ahmad, who had fled Iraq, came back to Baghdad. Five years later, he died in Tabriz (1410) in a battle led against the Turkmen Kara Koyunlu ("\[Those of the] Black Sheep"), who took Baghdad in 1412."<sup>[\[113\]](#cite_note-124)</sup>"Kara Koyunlu, also spelled Qara Qoyunlu, Turkish Karakoyunlular, English Black Sheep, Turkmen tribal federation that ruled Azerbaijan, Armenia, and Iraq from about 1375 to 1468."<sup>[\[114\]](#cite_note-125)</sup>

[^cite_note-163]: &#x20;"Āghā Muḥammad Khān remained nine days in the vicinity of Tiflis. His victory proclaimed the restoration of Iranian military power in the region formerly under Safavid domination."<sup>[\[150\]](#cite_note-162)</sup>

[^cite_note-175]: &#x20;"Griboedov not only extended protection to those Caucasian captives who sought to go home but actively promoted the return of even those who did not volunteer. Large numbers of Georgian and Armenian captives had lived in Iran since 1804 or as far back as 1795." <sup>[\[159\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Cambridge_History_of_Iran_Vol._7_Ch._9\[httpsbooksgooglecabooksidH20Xt157iYUCpgPA339_339]-172)</sup><sup>[\[160\]](#cite_note-173)</sup> Until the mid-fourteenth century, Armenians had constituted a majority in [Eastern Armenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Armenia).<sup>[\[161\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBournoutian198011,_13–4-174)</sup>

[^cite_note-177]: &#x20;"\[The Shah] deep inside understood that he would be unable to resist Sinan Pasha, i.e. the Sardar of Jalaloghlu, in a\[n open] battle. Therefore he ordered to relocate the whole population of Armenia - Christians, Jews and Muslims alike, to Persia, so that the Ottomans find the country depopulated."<sup>[\[162\]](#cite_note-176)</sup>

[^cite_note-185]: &#x20;The term "Tatars", employed by the Russians, referred to [Turkish-speaking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_languages) Muslims (Shia and [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam)) of [Transcaucasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcaucasia).<sup>[\[168\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBournoutian2020p._35_n._25-183)</sup> Unlike Armenians and [Georgians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgians), the Tatars did not have their own alphabet and used the [Perso-Arabic script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_alphabet).<sup>[\[168\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBournoutian2020p._35_n._25-183)</sup> After 1918 with the establishment of the [Azerbaijan Democratic Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijan_Democratic_Republic), and "especially during the [Soviet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) era", the Tatar group identified itself as "[Azerbaijani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijanis)".<sup>[\[168\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBournoutian2020p._35_n._25-183)</sup> Prior to 1918 the word "[Azerbaijan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijan_\(toponym\))" exclusively referred to the [Iranian province of Azarbayjan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijan_\(Iran\)).<sup>[\[169\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBournoutian2020xiv-184)</sup>
